Forking and invariant types in regular ordered Abelian groups Thanks: The author was partially supported by GeoMod AAPG2019 (ANR-DFG), Geometric and Combinatorial Configurations in Model Theory.
Akash HOSSAIN

Abstract
. We give a characterization of forking in regular ordered Abelian groups. In particular, we prove that C ​ \downfree f 𝐴 ​ B C\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B if and only if c ​ \downfree f 𝐴 ​ B c\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B for each singleton c ∈ dcl ​ ( A ​ C ) c\in\textup{dcl}(AC) in these structures.



Introduction

The non-forking global extensions of some unary type in the theory of divisible ordered Abelian groups (DOAG) are very easy to describe: they correspond to the invariant cuts, and there are at most two of them for any base parameter set. Now, if we look at a finite tuple
c=(c_{1},...,c_{n})
in DOAG, a necessary condition for
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
to be non-forking over
A
(
c_{1},...,c_{n}\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B
) is to have
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B
for every
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination
d
of the
c_{i}
. As the type in DOAG of a tuple is characterized by the cuts of the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combinations of its components, one can wonder whether this condition is sufficient. We show in this paper (
theorem

1.14
) that the answer is yes. Moreover, by using quantifier elimination in the Presburger language, we can extend our results to find a characterization of forking in the whole class of regular ordered Abelian groups (ROAG, see
definition

6.1
and
proposition

6.5
):

Theorem 1
.

Let
M\models
ROAG
,
A
,
B
parameter subsets of
M
, and
c=c_{1}...c_{n}\in M^{n}
. Let
\kappa=\max(|AB|,2^{\aleph_{0}})^{+}
, and suppose
{M}
is
\kappa
-saturated and strongly-
\kappa
-homogeneous. If
M
is discrete, interpret
1
as its least positive element, else interpret
1
as
0
(this is the standard interpretation of
1
in the Presburger language). Let
C
be the subgroup of
M
generated by
c
, and
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}
the relative divisible closures in
M
of the respective subgroups of
M
generated by
A\cup\{1\},AB\cup\{1\}
. Then
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B
if and only if
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{d}}B
, if and only if
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
admits a global
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A^{\prime}\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant extension in
S(M^{eq})
, if and only if the following conditions hold:

(1)

Every closed bounded interval of
B^{\prime}
that has a point in
C
already has a point in the divisible closure of
A^{\prime}
.

(2)

For all prime
l
, if
[M:lM]
is infinite, then we have
(C+l^{N}M)\cap(B^{\prime}+l^{N}M)=A^{\prime}+l^{N}M
for all
N>0
.

Moreover,
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
admits an
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension inside
S(M)
if and only if the above conditions hold, and, additionally, for every prime
l
for which
[M:lM]
is finite, we have
C\subseteq\bigcap\limits_{N>0}A^{\prime}+l^{N}M
.

This description of forking is the most simple and natural way to assemble together the (well-known) descriptions of forking in the theory of torsion-free Abelian groups, and in DLO. Our results clearly imply that forking in ROAG is a phenomenon that happens in dimension one: we have
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B
if and only if, for each singleton
c\in\textup{acl}(AC)
(which coincides here with
\textup{dcl}(AC)
), we have
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{f}}B
. Note that this particular property of unary forking is well-known in stable
1
-based theories. This raises the question of how this property relates to local modularity in, say, o-minimal theories. It also suggests that one might define over unstable theories a notion (satisfied by ROAG) which would extend that of
1
-basedness in the stable world. Note that we cannot hope to have this property in infinite (expansions of) fields, as it does not even hold in ACF.

Our main results rely heavily on our description of forking in DOAG, which really is the core of our paper. This theory is an enrichment of DLO and the theory of non-trivial
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces, and it is a reduct of RCF, three very common theories in which we know exactly what is forking. However, the current literature lacks of a satisfying description of forking in DOAG, and this paper fills that gap. Simon gave in (
[
11
]
, Proposition 2.5) a characterization of forking in dimension one (essentially the one we describe in the first condition of
1
) for dp-minimal ordered structures, which include dp-minimal ordered groups. One can also use Theorem 13.7 from
[
6
]
to establish our characterization of forking in DOAG in the particular case where the base parameter set is an Archimedean-complete model.

Dolich gave in
[
3
]
(see
[
13
]
for a survey) a characterization of forking in o-minimal expansions of real-closed fields. This very nice characterization naturally links forking with our intuition on o-minimal geometry. The independence notion introduced by Dolich is shown to be stronger or equal to forking in any o-minimal theory, but, for technical reasons, one needs to be in an expansion of RCF to prove equality. In these extensions, Dolich shows that their independence coincides with the abstract model-theoretic notion of “non-1-dividing in semi-simple theories” (see their Section 8). We do not know whether non-forking coincides with the independence of Dolich in DOAG, however we point out in subsection
1.4
that forking does coincide with 1-dividing.

Our work is very close in spirit to the work of Menunni and Hils-Menunni (
[
9
]
,
[
7
]
) on the domination monoid. They focus on a different notion, in a larger class of theories. However, while they describe the space of invariant types (over arbitrary small parameter sets) up to equidomination, we essentially study the space of
A
-invariant types (with
A
fixed) up to
A
-interdefinability. One new difficulty that comes with having to deal with
A
-invariant types instead of arbitrary invariant types is that we have to deal with
Archimedean extensions
, the analogue of immediate extensions for the Archimedean/convex valuation (see the end of page 58 of
[
5
]
for a formal definition).

This paper aims to be the first step towards a systematic characterization of forking in every complexity class of arbitrary ordered Abelian groups. Interesting future work could be to get a nice characterization of forking for dp-minimal ordered groups, as quantifier elimination is not very complicated in this class, and the case in dimension one is already covered by the work of Simon.

Let us give a brief outline of the proof of our description of forking in DOAG. Let
A
,
B
,
c
be as in
1
so that the conditions of the list hold. First of all, we show that
c
is
A
-interdefinable with a tuple
d
which is under “normal form”. After that, we split
d
into fibers of ad-hoc group valuations with respect to which being in normal form ensures that
d
is
separated
(see
definition

1.35
). For each subtuple
d^{\prime}
of this partition of
d
, we build a global
A
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d^{\prime}/AB)
. Finally, we show that we can “glue” all those extensions into a global
A
-invariant extension of the full type
\textup{tp}(d/AB)
, and we are done. We will try to be as explicit as possible in our manipulations, and we will show that our ad-hoc valuations interact in a meaningful way with the model-theoretic notions of tensor product and weak orthogonality, which play an important role in
[
9
]
,
[
7
]
.

In the first section of this paper, we write basic definitions, and we give a more detailed and formalized outline of the proof of our result in DOAG.

The way we build our global invariant extension is typically a bottom-up approach. However, the concepts have to be introduced in a particular order to be coherent, and it will be more convenient for us to present the steps of this process in a top-down order:

In the second section, we define our ad-hoc valuations, and we show, given global
A
-invariant extensions of the
\textup{tp}(d^{\prime}/AB)
, how to glue them into a global
A
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d/AB)
.

In the third section, we show how to build a global
A
-invariant extension of each of the
\textup{tp}(d^{\prime}/AB)
.

In the fourth section, we show how to build the normal form
d
from
c
. The transformation of a family into a family under normal form is carried out in a very procedural way which goes through many steps, where we apply succesively
A
-translations and maps from
\textup{GL}^{n}(\mathbb{Q})
to
c
. Once
d
is built, our characterization of forking for DOAG will be established.

We extend our results in DOAG to ROAG in the fifth section. We use quantifier elimination to describe the types, their global extensions and their conjugates. We end the section by proving our main result,
theorem

8.1
, where we give our characterization of forking for ROAG.

Let us also describe the outline of the proof. In ROAG, we can see a complete type as a reunion of countably many partial types (
\Phi
-types) which are “independent” to each other (see
lemma

6.2
). For each such partial type, we give algebraic characterizations (essentially the conditions of
1
) of when there exists a partial global extension which does not fork over
A
, and when this extension is
A
-invariant. Our results in DOAG allow us to deal with partial types that involve equality and order. The results about the other partial types may already follow from literature on the model theory of torsion free Abelian groups, we still prove them explicitly for completeness.

1.
Forking in DOAG

1.1.
Cut-independence

Notation

In the theory of some total order
<
, a set is considered an interval (be it closed, open, half-open, possibly with infinite bounds) when its lower bound is smaller or equal to its upper bound. For instance, if we consider, say an interval
]a,b[
, then it is implicit that
a\leqslant b
.

Assumptions 1.1
.

Let
(M,<,...)
be the expansion of an infinite totally ordered first-order structure. Let
C\supseteq A\subseteq B
be parameter subsets of
M
. Suppose
M
is
|AB|^{+}
-saturated and strongly-
|AB|^{+}
-homogeneous.

In this paper, we will manipulate various linear orders (not only ordered Abelian groups, but also the chains of their convex subgroups, and other ad-hoc orders). In these linear orders, the cuts will be seen as type-definable sets. The formal definition is as follows:

Definition 1.2
.

Let
c\in M
. The
cut
of
c
over
A
will be the
A
-type-definable set
\textup{ct}(c/A)
, defined as the intersection of every interval containing
c
with bounds in
A\cup\{\pm\infty\}
(a singleton is a closed interval).

Let
P
be some partition of
M
into convex sets. Then there is a natural linear order on
P
, the only linear order that makes the projection
M\longrightarrow P
an increasing map.

The set of cuts over
A
is clearly a partition of
M
into convex sets, therefore the above paragraph applies.

Let us also write
x>A
when
\forall a\in A,\ x>a
. If
\emptyset\neq A^{\prime}\subseteq A
, define
\textup{ct}_{>}(A^{\prime}/A)
(resp.
\textup{ct}_{<}(A^{\prime}/A)
) as the cut over
A
that corresponds to the elements that are
>A^{\prime}
(resp.
<A^{\prime}
), and strictly smaller (resp. larger) than any element of
A
that is
>A^{\prime}
(resp.
<A^{\prime}
). More generally, if
X
is some (type/
\vee
)-definable subset of
M
, we write
\textup{ct}_{<}(X/A)=\textup{ct}_{<}(X(A)/A)
, and similarly for
\textup{ct}_{>}(X/A)
.

A
A^{\prime}
\textup{ct}_{<}(A^{\prime}/A)
\textup{ct}_{>}(A^{\prime}/A)

Definition 1.3
.

Let us define the following independence notions, which are ternary relations defined on the small subsets of
M
:

•

\underset{}{\downfree^{d}}
is the standard non-dividing independence (see
[
14
]
, Definition 7.1.2).

•

\underset{}{\downfree^{f}}
is the standard non-forking independence (see
[
14
]
, Definition 7.1.7).

•

Let
c_{1}...c_{n}\in M
. Define
c_{1}...c_{n}\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
when
\textup{tp}(c_{1}...c_{n}/AB)
admits a global (in
S(M)
)
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension. Define
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
when
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
for each finite tuple
c
of
C
.

•

\underset{}{\downfree^{bo}}
(for “Bounded Orbit”) is a weaker notion than
\underset{}{\downfree^{inv}}
, where we require the orbit under
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
of the global extension to have a bounded cardinal instead of being a single point.

Fact 1.4
.

The inclusions
\underset{}{\downfree^{inv}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{bo}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{f}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{d}}
always hold in any first-order structure.

Remark 1.5
.

Note that in DOAG, if
A
is not included in
\{0\}
, then
\textup{dcl}(A)
is a model. In an o-minimal theory (in fact in any NIP theory),
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
,
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}}
and
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
all coincide over models, and one can easily check by hand that it is also the case in DOAG for
A\subseteq\{0\}
. More precisely, the fact that
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}}
follows from (
[
1
]
, Theorem 1.1), while
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
over models trivially follows from the fact that non-forking coincides with the independence notion given by Lascar-invariance (see for instance
[
8
]
, Proposition 2.1). As a result, the abstract equality
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
is already well-known in DOAG, though this equality does not help in any way to relate forking with more concrete geometric phenomena.

We show in
theorem

8.1
that
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
,
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}}
and
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}}
all coincide in regular groups.

Now, we are looking at these abstract model-theoretic notions in ordered Abelian groups, which are structures that come with much more concrete geometric notions (the atomic definable sets are the solution sets of
\mathbb{Q}
-linear equations and inequations with parameters). The theory DOAG is even o-minimal. Thus we expect to find a purely geometric description of our abstract independence relations. The most basic example of a dependence behavior is the following:

Example 1.6
.

Suppose
M\models\textup{DLO}
, and identify some elementary substructure of
M
with
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
A=\emptyset
,
B=\{0,2\}
,
C=\{1\}
. Then
\textup{acl}(AC)\cap\textup{acl}(AB)=\textup{acl}(A)=A
, but
C\underset{A}{{\not\downfree}^{\mathbf{d}}}B
. Indeed, if we define
I_{n}=\left[4n,4n+2\right]
, then the
(I_{n})_{n<\omega}
are pairwise-disjoint and
A
-conjugates, thus they divide over
A
, and
1\in I_{0}
.

In this example, the definable set that forks is an interval which is disjoint from some conjugates. We can see with the same reasoning that such intervals always divide:

Lemma 1.7
.

In the expansion of some total order, let
I
be an interval with non-empty interior such that its bounds are
A
-conjugates. Then
I
divides over
A
.

Corollary 1.8
.

Suppose that the theory of
M
is o-minimal, and let
I=[b_{1},b_{2}]
be an interval which is closed, bounded and disjoint from
\textup{dcl}(A)
(in particular,
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are
A
-conjugates). Then
I
divides over
A
.

Note that we only consider bounded intervals: the bounds are not in
\{\pm\infty\}
, for they must be
A
-conjugates. Those considerations allow us to characterize forking in dimension one:

Proposition 1.9
.

Suppose the theory of
M
is o-minimal. Then, for every singleton
c\in M
, we have
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
if and only if every closed interval with bounds in
\textup{dcl}(AB)
containing
c
has a point in
\textup{dcl}(A)
.

Proof.

The left-to-right implication is a consequence of
corollary

1.8
and
1.4
. For the other direction, assume the condition on the right holds. Then the elements of
\textup{dcl}(AB)
having the same cut as
c
must all be either strictly smaller or strictly larger than
c
. Then, either
\textup{ct}_{<}(\textup{ct}(c/\textup{dcl}(A))/M)
or
\textup{ct}_{>}(\textup{ct}(c/\textup{dcl}(A))/M)
corresponds to a global (and complete by o-minimality) unary type that extends
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
. As
\textup{ct}(c/\textup{dcl}(A))
is
A
-invariant, so are both those types, and we get the proposition.∎

A
M
B
c
b_{1}
b_{2}
invariant cuts

Definition 1.10
.

Let
R
be
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Q}
. Suppose
M
is an expansion of some
R
-module, and let
f
be some
n
-ary
\emptyset
-definable function in this structure. We will write
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(R)
when there exist
\lambda_{1}...\lambda_{n}\in R
so that
f
coincides with the function
x_{1}...x_{n}\longmapsto\sum\limits_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}
. We will write
\textup{LC}(R)
when
n
is implicit.

Assumptions 1.11
.

Work in DOAG, in the language of ordered
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces. In this language, every substructure is a definably closed
\mathbb{Q}
-vector-subspace. The independence notions we are looking at are insensitive to definable closure (i.e. we have
C\underset{A}{\downfree}B
if and only if
\textup{dcl}(AC)\underset{\textup{dcl}(A)}{\downfree}\textup{dcl}(AB)
), so we fix
C\geqslant A\leqslant B

\mathbb{Q}
-vector subspaces of
M
.

It is well-known that DOAG is complete, has quantifier elimination and is o-minimal in this language. Moreover, for
c_{1}...c_{n},d_{1}...d_{n}\in M
, we have :

(c_{i})_{i}\equiv_{A}(d_{i})_{i}\Longleftrightarrow\forall f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q}),\ \textup{ct}\left(f(c)/A\right)=\textup{ct}\left(f(d)/A\right)

By
proposition

1.9
, one can see that
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
if and only if for all
c_{1}...c_{n}\in C
, there exists
p
a global extension of
\textup{tp}(c_{1}...c_{n}/B)
so that for all
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q})
, for all closed interval
I
with bounds in
M
not disjoint from
A
, we have
p(x)\models f(x)\not\in I
.

The above description of
\underset{}{\downfree^{inv}}
is still not geometric, because it is existential in
p\in S(M)
. There is a geometric description of a weaker version of this, where we swap the first two quantifiers:

Definition 1.12
.

Define
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{cut}}B
if the following equivalent conditions hold:

•

\forall c_{1}...c_{n}\in C,\ \forall f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q}),\ \left(f(c)\right)\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
(the quantifier over
p
has been swapped with the one over
f
).

•

\forall c\in C,\ c\underset{A}{\downfree^{inv}}B
.

•

Every closed interval with bounds in
B
that has a point in
C
already has a point in
A
.

For
c_{1}...c_{n}\in M
, we define
c_{1}...c_{n}\underset{A}{\downfree^{cut}}B
when
(A+\mathbb{Q}c_{1}+...+\mathbb{Q}c_{n})\underset{A}{\downfree^{cut}}B
.

The last item of
definition

1.12
is a purely geometric description which depends only on
A
,
B
,
C
. This description is simple and uses very little algebra: we have to replace
A
(resp.
B
,
C
) by the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector subspace generated by
A
(resp.
AB
,
AC
) as done in the notation, then we take every point from
C
, we completely ignore the algebraic relations between those points, and we check for each point a condition that only depends on the linear order.

Lemma 1.13
.

We have
\underset{}{\downfree^{d}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{cut}}
in
DOAG
.

Proof.

See
corollary

1.8
.
∎

It actually turns out that this nice, but weak independence notion
\underset{}{\downfree^{cut}}
is no weaker than
\underset{}{\downfree^{inv}}
in DOAG. This is the fundamental result of this paper:

Theorem 1.14
.

In
DOAG
, we have
\underset{}{\downfree^{inv}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{cut}}
.

By
1.4
and
lemma

1.13
, we just need to prove that
\underset{}{\downfree^{cut}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{inv}}
.

Assumptions 1.15
.

Recall from
1.11
that
B\geqslant A\leqslant C
are
\mathbb{Q}
-vector-subspaces of
M
. We assume
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{cut}}B
. Let
c_{1}...c_{n}\in C
.

Our goal is to build a global
A
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(c_{1}...c_{n}/B)
. This will be achieved in sections
2
,
3
,
4
, and we deal with ROAG and examples in the remaining sections.

Remark 1.16
.

If
c
is a singleton from
C\setminus A
, then we have
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
if and only if at least one of the following conditions holds:

•

Every bounded
B
-definable closed interval included in
\textup{ct}(c/A)
(viewed as an
A
-type-definable set) has all its points smaller than
c
.

•

Every bounded
B
-definable closed interval included in
\textup{ct}(c/A)
has all its points larger than
c
.

Definition 1.17
.

If the first condition of
remark

1.16
holds, then we say that
c

leans right
with respect to
A
and
B
. If the second condition holds, then
c

leans left
. The two conditions hold at the same time if and only if
\textup{ct}(c/\textup{dcl}(A))
has no point in
\textup{dcl}(AB)
.

A
left-leaning
B
cut-dependent
B
right-leaning
A

It is easy to see that
c
leans right (resp. left) with respect to
A
and
B
if and only if there exists
A^{\prime}\subseteq A
so that
\textup{ct}(c/B)=\textup{ct}_{>}(A^{\prime}/B)
(resp.
\textup{ct}_{<}(A^{\prime}/B)
).

This definition also makes sense (and will be used) in more abstract linear orders that do not come from ordered groups.

1.2.
Valuations

The content of this subsection is well-known in the literature, and most of the proofs are omitted.

Assumptions 1.18
.

In this subsection, let
G
be an Abelian group.

Definition 1.19
.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G
, and
\Gamma
a linearly-ordered set with a least element
-\infty
. A map
\textup{val}:\ G\longrightarrow\Gamma
is an
H
-valuation
if it satisfies the following axioms for all
x,y\in G
:

•

\textup{val}(x-y)\leqslant\max(\textup{val}(x),\textup{val}(y))

•

\textup{val}(x)=-\infty\Longleftrightarrow x\in H

A
valuation
over
G
is a
\{0\}
-valuation.

If
\textup{val}^{\prime}
is another
H
-valuation of
G
, we say that
\textup{val}^{\prime}

refines

val
if there exists an increasing map
f:\ \textup{val}^{\prime}(G)\longrightarrow\textup{val}(G)
so that
\textup{val}=\textup{val}^{\prime}\circ f
.

Equivalently, an
H
-valuation can be seen as a valuation over
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{H}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{H}$}}}
.

Remark 1.20
.

Let us remark for the readers unfamiliar with valuations that, if
\textup{val}(x)<\textup{val}(y)
, then it follows from the axioms that
\textup{val}(x+y)=\textup{val}(y)
.

The next lemma gives us a canonical way to build an
H
-valuation from a preorder over
G
satisfying certain conditions:

Lemma 1.21
.

Let
P
be a preorder over
G
. Let
\sim
be the associated equivalence relation over
G
,
\pi
the quotient map, and
<
the associated order on
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}
. Suppose
<
is linear, and we have
\pi(x)<\pi(y)\Longrightarrow\pi(x+y)=\pi(y)
for all
x,y\in G
(the same relation as in
remark

1.20
). Then the following conditions hold:

•

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\displaystyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.83437pt}{$\textstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.34427pt}{$\scriptstyle{\sim}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-0.99101pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\sim}$}}}
has a least element, which is
\pi(0)
.

•

The fiber
\pi^{-1}(\pi(0))
is a subgroup of
G
.

•

\pi
is a
\pi^{-1}(\pi(0))
-valuation.

For example, if
P
is the divisibility relation on an integral local ring
R
(
P(x,y)
when
y
divides
x
), then
P
is a preorder satisfying the axiom
\pi(x)<\pi(y)\Longrightarrow\pi(x+y)=\pi(y)
of
lemma

1.21
. In that case,
R/\sim
is in natural correspondence with the poset of principal ideals of
R
, and
<
is linear if and only if
R
is a valuation ring. Then, the group valuation given by the proposition is the natural ring valuation on
R
.

Assumptions 1.22
.

Suppose now
G
is an ordered Abelian group.

Proposition 1.23
.

Let
x,y\in G
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

x\in\bigcup\limits_{n<\omega}\left[-n|y|,n|y|\right]
.

(2)

For every convex subgroup
H
of
G
, if
y\in H
, then
x\in H
.

(3)

The convex subgroup of
G
generated by
x
is included in the one generated by
y
.

Definition 1.24
.

These equivalent conditions define a relation on
G
which is clearly a preorder that satisfies the necessary conditions of
lemma

1.21
, with
\pi^{-1}(\pi(0))=\{0\}
. This gives a valuation over
G
, which we call
\Delta
, the
Archimedean valuation
. For
x\in G
,
\Delta^{-1}(\Delta(x))
is called the
Archimedean class
of
x
. By abuse of notation, we may identify the Archimedean value of an element with its Archimedean class.

The idea behind the statement
\Delta(x)<\Delta(y)
is that
x
is “infinitesimal” compared to
y
, or that
y
is “infinitely greater” than
x
.

Equivalently:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta(x)<\Delta(y)&\Longleftrightarrow&x\in\bigcap\limits_{n>0}\left]-\dfrac{1}{n}|y|,\dfrac{1}{n}|y|\right[\\
&&\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&|y|\in\bigcap\limits_{n>0}\left]n|x|,+\infty\right[\end{array}

Let us draw a picture (not scaled correctly) which intuitively shows how the Archimedean classes look like. Suppose
G
has exactly four Archimedean classes
\Delta(0)<\delta_{1}<\delta_{2}<\delta_{3}
. Then
G
looks like this:
\delta_{3}
\delta_{3}
\delta_{2}
\delta_{2}
\delta_{1}
0

There is no standard terminology in the modern literature when it comes to the Archimedean valuation and related notions. Some use the keyword “convex” to refer to all those notions, others use the very obscure names “
i
-extension” or “
i
-completeness”. We draw our inspiration from older sources: we choose to use the terminology of
[
5
]
, which, on top of being a good introductory paper, sets up notations which we find more intuitive and easier to work with.

Remark 1.25
.

A convex subgroup
H\leqslant G
is equal to the union of the convex subgroups of
G
generated by each
x\in H
. So
H
is entirely determined by its direct image
\Delta(H)
, which is an initial segment of
\Delta(G)
. We can note that the set
\mathcal{C}(G)
of convex subgroups of
G
is totally ordered by inclusion, and that
H\longmapsto\Delta(H)
is an order isomorphism between
\mathcal{C}(G)
and the set of non-empty initial segments of
\Delta(G)
ordered by inclusion.

We can also note that the cosets of some convex subgroup
H\leqslant G
are all convex, because the translations are strictly increasing. So the quotient of
G
by some convex subgroup
H
is naturally endowed with a total order, as defined in
definition

1.2
.

Definition 1.26
.

Let
H
be a subgroup of
G
, and
\delta\in\Delta(G)
. We define
H_{<\delta}=\left\{x\in H|\Delta(x)<\delta\right\}
. We introduce a similar notation for the conditions
\leqslant\delta
,
=\delta
…More generally, if
P\subseteq\Delta(G)
, we define
H_{\in P}=\left\{x\in H|\Delta(x)\in P\right\}
.

1.3.
Outline of the proof

Although
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}
and
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
are very combinatorial notions, we have to set up a rather technical algebraic machinery to prove that they coincide. Let us explain in this subsection what we do conceptually.

Assumptions 1.27
.

On top of
1.15
, we assume that
M
is
|B|^{+}
-saturated and strongly
|B|^{+}
-homogeneous.

We wish to show
c_{1}\ldots c_{n}\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
. For this, we partition our family
c
into smaller subfamilies, that we call “blocks”, and we build a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of the type of each block. We get a family of global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant types, that we call (in
definition

1.34
) a “block extension”, and what is left for us is to “glue” those types into a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of the full type of
c
.

Definition 1.28
.

Let
p_{1}\ldots p_{n}\in S(A)
. We say that the family
(p_{i})_{i}
is
weakly orthogonal
if
\bigcup\limits_{i}p_{i}(x_{i})
is a complete type over
A
in the variables
x_{1}\ldots x_{n}
.

There is a link between orthogonality and convex subgroups that appears in (
[
9
]
, Proposition 4.5). We state a variant:

Lemma 1.29
.

Let
G
be some
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant convex subgroup of
M
, and
c,d\in M
. If
c\in(G+A)\not\ni d
, then
\textup{tp}(c/A)
and
\textup{tp}(d/A)
are weakly orthogonal.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that they are not. Then
\textup{tp}(d/A)
is not complete in
S(Ac)
, i.e.
\textup{ct}(d/A)
has a point in
A+\mathbb{Q}\cdot c
, and this point is in
A+G
. By strong homogeneity, there exists an automorhpism
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}(M/A)
such that
\sigma(d)\in A+G
. As
G
is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant, we have
d\in A+G
, a contradiction.
∎

Note that
lemma

1.29
holds in the more general context of a relatively divisible subgroup
G
in the expansion of an Abelian group which is in some sense
algebraically bounded
, that is when the definable closure coincides with the relative divisible closure of the generated subgroup.

Example 1.30
.

Suppose
A
is the lexicographical product:

\mathbb{Q}\times_{lex}\mathbb{Q}\times_{lex}\mathbb{Q}\leqslant\mathbb{R}\times_{lex}\mathbb{R}\times_{lex}\mathbb{R}

let
c_{1}=(0,0,\sqrt{2})
,
c_{2}=(\sqrt{2},0,0)
,
c_{3}\in M
such that:

c_{3}\in\bigcap\limits_{N>0}\left](0,N,0),\left(\dfrac{1}{N},0,0\right)\right[

let
G
be the convex subgroup
\bigcap\limits_{N>0}\left]\left(-\dfrac{1}{N},0,0\right),\left(\dfrac{1}{N},0,0\right)\right[
. Then we can show by
lemma

1.29
that
\textup{tp}(c_{2}/A)
is weakly orthogonal to both
\textup{tp}(c_{1}/A)
and
\textup{tp}(c_{3}/A)
using
G
.

Actually, we need not choose
G
to show that the type of
c_{1}
is weakly orthogonal to that of
c_{2}
, we may choose instead, for instance, the convex subgroup
\bigcap\limits_{N>0}\left]\left(0,-\dfrac{1}{N},0\right),\left(0,\dfrac{1}{N},0\right)\right[
. In fact, this works for any
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant convex subgroup of
G
which properly extends the convex subgroup
\bigcap\limits_{N>0}\left]\left(0,0,-\dfrac{1}{N}\right),\left(0,0,\dfrac{1}{N}\right)\right[
.

The same cannot be said for
c_{3}
,
G
seems to be the only valid witness for orthogonality. Indeed,
c_{2}
belongs to
H=\bigcup\limits_{N<\omega}\left[\left(-N,0,0\right),\left(N,0,0\right)\right]
, which is the least
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant convex subgroup properly extending
G
, whereas
c_{3}\not\in A+H^{\prime}
, with
H^{\prime}=\bigcup\limits_{N<\omega}\left[\left(0,-N,0\right),\left(0,N,0\right)\right]
the largest
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant proper convex subgroup of
G
(note that
H^{\prime}
witnesses orthogonality between
c_{3}
and
c_{1}
).

We conclude that the type of
c_{2}
is weakly orthogonal to the two other types, but for very different reasons. As it is harder to prove weak orthogonality between
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
,
c_{2}
is, in some sense that will be made formal in the next section, more related to
c_{3}
than it is to
c_{1}
.

Contrary to DLO, there seems to be “several layers” of weak orthogonality in DOAG. We understand them by defining several ways to partition our tuple, that refine each other. The finer the partition, the easier it becomes to find a block extension, the harder it is to “glue” the elements of the block extension.

In model theory, the standard way to “glue” global invariant types is via the tensor product:

Definition 1.31
.

Let
p,q
be
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant global types. We define the
tensor product
of
p
by
q
to be the following
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant complete global type:

p(x)\otimes q(y)=\left\{\varphi(x,y,m)|m\in M,\ \exists a\models q_{|Am},\ p(x)\models\varphi(x,a,m)\right\}

The tensor product is associative, but not commutative in general. However, two types that are weakly orthogonal must necessarily commute.

Fact 1.32
.

In
DOAG
(in fact in any distal theory, see
[
12
]
, Proposition 2.17), two global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant types that commute must be weakly orthogonal.

Lemma 1.33
.

Let
F_{1},F_{2}
be closed subspaces of
S(A)
. Suppose
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are weakly orthogonal for all
p_{i}\in F_{i}
. Then the set
F_{1}\times F_{2}
, seen as a topological subspace of
S(A)
, is exactly the topological direct product
F_{1}\times_{Top}F_{2}
, via the homeomorphism:

h:\ p_{1}(x_{1})\cup p_{2}(x_{2})\longmapsto(p_{1}(x_{1}),p_{2}(x_{2}))

Proof.

The map
h
is clearly a continuous bijection between compact separated spaces, thus it is a homeomorphism.
∎

The way we partition our tuple is with fibers of some ad-hoc valuations:

Definition 1.34
.

Let
val
be a
B
-valuation over
M
. Given our family
c
, the
val
-blocks
of
c
are defined as the maximal subtuples of
c
of elements of equal value. They form a partition of
c
.

By abuse of notation, if
c_{i}=(c_{ij})_{j}
is a
val
-block of
c
, we define
\textup{val}(c_{i})=\textup{val}(c_{ij})
, which does not depend on the choice of
j
.

A
weak
val
-block extension
of
c
is a family
(p_{i})_{i}
of global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extensions of the types over
B
of each
val
-block of
c
. Such a block extension is
strong
when
\bigcup\limits_{i}p_{i}
is consistent with
\textup{tp}(c/B)
.

In this paper, whenever we use the terminology of block extensions, it will always be for global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extensions of types over
B
, the parameter sets
A
,
B
will not change.

In particular, if
\textup{val}^{\prime}
refines
val
, then the
\textup{val}^{\prime}
-blocks of some family form a finer partition than its
val
-blocks.

Definition 1.35
.

Let
val
be an
A
-valuation over
M
. We say that the family
c
is
val
-separated
when the following conditions hold:

•

\forall(\lambda_{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{Q},\ \textup{val}\left(\sum\limits_{i}\lambda_{i}c_{i}\right)=\max_{i}(\lambda_{i}c_{i})
.

•

\forall i,\ c_{i}\not\in A
.

The notion of separatedness is an algebraic way to state that the
val
-blocks of a tuple are independent from each other. One can note that
c
is
val
-separated if and only if each of its
val
-blocks is. Moreover, a finite family that is separated with respect to some
A
-valuation must be a lift of some
\mathbb{Q}
-free family of
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}}}
, i.e. a family in
M
which maps via the canonical surjection to a
\mathbb{Q}
-free family in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}}}
. In particular, such a family is
\mathbb{Q}
-free.

Remark 1.36
.

The family
c
is clearly
A
-interdefinable with the lift of a
\mathbb{Q}
-free family from
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}}}
. Moreover, if
d
is a tuple that is
A
-interdefinable with
c
, then one can easily show that we have
c\underset{A}{\downfree}B
if and only if
d\underset{A}{\downfree}B
for
\underset{}{\downfree}\in\{\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}\}
.

Assumptions 1.37
.

By
remark

1.36
, on top of
1.27
, we may assume that
c
is a lift of a
\mathbb{Q}
-free family from
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}}}
.

Remark 1.38
.

Now, if
d
is another lift of a
\mathbb{Q}
-free family from
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{C}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}}}
, then
c
and
d
are
A
-interdefinable if and only if they have the same size
n
, and there exists
f\in\textup{GL}_{n}(\mathbb{Q})
such that the
i
-th component of
d
is an
A
-translate of the
i
-th component of
f(c)
for each
i
.

We just defined several ways to state that the blocks of some tuple are “independent” from each other and thus “easy to glue”. The fact that the types of the blocks are weakly orthogonal is a model-theoretic way to define this idea, whereas the notion of separatedness with respect to some valuation is more algebraic. The core of our proofs relies on building valuations over
M
for which these notions interact in a meaningful way.

More precisely, we build
D
-valuations
\textup{val}^{i}_{D}
on
M
, with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and with
D\leqslant M
, such that:

(C1)

\textup{val}^{3}_{D}
refines
\textup{val}^{2}_{D}
, which refines
\textup{val}^{1}_{D}
.

(C2)

\textup{val}^{3}_{\{0\}}=\Delta
and
\textup{val}^{2}_{\{0\}}
is trivial (it is refined by any valuation).

(C3)

if
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, then
c
is
A
-interdefinable with a family
d
that is simultaneously separated with respect to
\textup{val}^{3}_{A}
and
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
.

(C4)

If
d
is a
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-separated family, and
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, then
d
admits a strong
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-block extension.

(C5)

Given
d
a
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-separated family, and
(p_{i})_{i}
a strong
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-block extension of
d
, some tensor product of the
(p_{i})_{i}
is a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d/B)
.

(C6)

Suppose
d
is
\textup{val}^{2}_{D}
-separated, and let
(b_{i})_{i}
be its
\textup{val}^{2}_{D}
-blocks. Then
(\textup{tp}(b_{i}/D))_{i}
is weakly orthogonal. In particular, any weak
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
-block extension is strong.

(C7)

Given
d
a
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-separated family, and
(p_{i})_{i}
a weak (thus strong by the previous point)
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-block extension of
d
,
(p_{i})_{i}
is weakly orthogonal, and thus they commute.

Now, if we could build such valuations, then we would have
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
, which would finish the proof of
theorem

1.14
. Indeed, if
d
was given by
(C3)
, then by
remark

1.36
it would be enough to show that
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
. Now, again by
remark

1.36
, we would have
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, so
(C4)
would give us a strong
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-block extension of
d
, and
(C5)
would give us a way to glue it into a witness of
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
, which would conclude the proof. Moreover,
(C6)
, and
(C7)
would give us a fine understanding of the global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extensions of the type of
d
. In fact, we establish in
section

3
an exhaustive classification of the Stone space of all these global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extensions.

Last but far from least, what remains for us to do is to actually define explicitly those valuations
\textup{val}^{i}_{D}
, and to show that all those nice properties hold.

1.4.
Relation to Dolich-independence

We make short comments on how our result on DOAG relates to the work of Dolich
[
3
]
, their Section 8 in particular. Dolich defines in their paper a geometric independence notion in terms of “halfway-definable cells”, which we call
\underset{}{\downfree^{\textup{Dolich}}}
. It is shown that it coincides with
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
in any o-minimal expansion of RCF. To motivate their results, Dolich also gives five axioms for independence notions (allegedly from unpublished notes of Shelah), four of which are always satisfied by non-forking in any theory, and the fifth, called “Chain Condition”, is a weakening of the independence theorem. They also define an independence notion called “non-1-dividing”, with a combinatorial definition which strengthens that of non-forking ; let us write it
\underset{}{\downfree^{1-\mathbf{d}}}
. They claim that, in case some independence relation satisfies the five axioms in a given theory, non-1-dividing is the weakest relation satisfying those axioms, and they show that
\underset{}{\downfree^{\textup{Dolich}}}
satisfies the five axioms in any o-minimal theory. In particular, we have
\underset{}{\downfree^{\textup{Dolich}}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{1-\mathbf{d}}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
in any o-minimal theory, and those inclusions are equalities in expansions of RCF.

Note that, if we generalize the independence notion
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
to any o-minimal theory by the definition: “any closed bounded interval with bounds in
\textup{dcl}(AB)
having a point in
\textup{dcl}(AC)
already has a point in
\textup{dcl}(A)
”, then it follows from
corollary

1.8
that
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}
, hence in particular
\underset{}{\downfree^{\textup{Dolich}}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}
. This inclusion is strict in general, for in RCF, one may find an example where
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
holds, while
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{alg}}}B
fails, which implies
C\underset{A}{{\not\downfree}^{\textup{Dolich}}}B
. So the main difference between the geometric independence notion introduced by Dolich and ours is that ours is easily shown to be weaker than non-forking in the general case, and the difficulties come when we prove that it is actually as strong as non-forking in DOAG, while
\underset{}{\downfree^{\textup{Dolich}}}
is clearly stronger than non-forking, and it is difficult to prove the other direction in RCF.

While we do not know whether
\underset{}{\downfree^{\textup{Dolich}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
in DOAG (it would be a strengthening of our result), we remark that
\underset{}{\downfree^{1-\mathbf{d}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
in any theory (DOAG in particular) where
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
. Indeed, one may easily show that
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
satisfies the Chain Condition, using the fact that it satisfies Extension. If
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
, then it follows that
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
satisfies the five axioms. By maximality of
\underset{}{\downfree^{1-\mathbf{d}}}
, we have
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}\subseteq\underset{}{\downfree^{1-\mathbf{d}}}
, and the other inclusion always holds.

2.
How to glue types via ad-hoc valuations

In this section, we define the valuations
\textup{val}^{i}_{A}
(
i\in\{1,2,3\}
), and we show that they satisfy the gluing properties
(C5)
,
(C6)
,
(C7)
. We also give a classification of the cuts over some parameter set. We work with
1.27
.

2.1.
Basic definitions and classification of the cuts

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
c
,
d\in M
, and
a\in A
, such that
\textup{ct}(c/A)=\textup{ct}(d/A)
. Then
\textup{ct}(c+a/A)=\textup{ct}(d+a/A)
.

Proof.

If not, then there exists
a^{\prime}\in A
which lies strictly between
c+a
and
d+a
.
d+a
c+a
a^{\prime}

then
a^{\prime}-a\in A
lies strictly between
c
and
d
, contradicting the hypothesis.∎
c+a
d+a
a^{\prime}
a^{\prime}-a
d
c

We hope the figures make the proofs easier to understand. However, we do not want them to be misleading, thus we would like to say that they may not cover all the possible cases. For instance, if
a
was negative, then the correct picture would be reversed.

Definition 2.2
.

By
lemma

2.1
(and by saturation),
A
acts by translation over the set of all the cuts of
M
over
A
. For
c\in M
, denote by
\textup{Stab}(c/A)
the stabilizer of
\textup{ct}(c/A)
.

Note that such a stabilizer is only a subgroup of
A
, which is clearly convex in
A
, but not in
M
.

Definition 2.3
.

Let
d\in M
. If
d\not\in A
, then we define:

G(d/A)=\cap\left\{\left]-|a|,|a|\right[\ :\ a\in A\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/A)\right\}

else we define
G(d/A)=\{0\}
. Either way, we also define:

H(d/A)=\cup\left\{\left[-|a|,|a|\right]\ :\ a\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)\right\}

We view
G(d/A)
as an
A
-type-definable set, and
H(d/A)
as an
A
-
\vee
-definable set. They have the same points in
A
, however they do not have the same points in
M
when
d\not\in A
. They are
A
-(type/
\vee
)-definable convex subgroups.

Example 2.4
.

In
example

1.30
,
G(c_{2}/A)=G(c_{3}/A)=G
, and
H(c_{2}/A)=H(c_{3}/A)=H^{\prime}
. However,
G(c_{1}/A)
is the group of elements which are infinitesimal with respect to
A
, and it is distinct from
G
. As for
H(c_{1}/A)
, it is trivial.

Likewise, for arbitrary
A
, if
\Delta(0)<\Delta(d)<\Delta(A\setminus\{0\})
(i.e.
d
is infinitesimal with respect to
A
), then
H(d/A)
is trivial, and
G(d/A)
is the type-definable group of elements which are infinitesimal with respect to
A
.

If
\Delta(d)>\Delta(A)
, then
H(d/A)
is the convex subgroup generated by
A
, whereas
G(d/A)
is the whole group.

Remark 2.5
.

We always have
G(d/A)\geqslant H(d/A)
. By definition,
H(d/A)
is the convex subgroup generated by
\textup{Stab}(d/A)
, i.e. the least
A
-
\vee
-definable convex subgroup containing
\textup{Stab}(d/A)
. In case
d\not\in A
,
G(d/A)
is the largest
A
-type-definable convex subgroup disjoint from
A\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/A)
. For all
d
,
d^{\prime}\in M
, we have in fact :

G(d/A)=G(d^{\prime}/A)\Longrightarrow H(d/A)=H(d^{\prime}/A)

the only case where the implication is not an equivalence being when one point is in
A
, and the other is infinitesimal with respect to
A
. We also have:

\textup{Stab}(d/A)=\textup{Stab}(d^{\prime}/A)\Longleftrightarrow H(d/A)=H(d^{\prime}/A).

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
d,d^{\prime}\in M
. If
G(d/A)<G(d^{\prime}/A)
, then we have
G(d/A)<H(d^{\prime}/A)
.

Proof.

By definition of
G(d/A)
, there must exist
a\in A\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/A)
such that
a\in G(d^{\prime}/A)
. By
remark

2.5
,
a\in H(d^{\prime}/A)\setminus G(d/A)
.
∎

Let us now build
\textup{val}^{1}_{A}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2}\in M
. Then, with respect to inclusion, we have
G(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)\leqslant\max\left(G(d_{1}/A),G(d_{2}/A)\right)
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that
G(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)
strictly contains
\max\left(G(d_{1}/A),G(d_{2}/A)\right)
. Then, there must exist
a\in\textup{Stab}(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)
such that
a\not\in\textup{Stab}(d_{i}/A)
. As
\textup{Stab}(d_{i}/A)
is a convex subgroup of
A
, we also have
\dfrac{a}{2}\not\in\textup{Stab}(d_{i}/A)
, so there exists
a_{i}\in A
which lies strictly between
d_{i}
and
d_{i}+\dfrac{a}{2}
.
d_{1}

.∎

Definition 2.8
.

By
lemma

2.7
, the map
x\longmapsto G(x/A)
is an
A
-valuation, so we set
\textup{val}^{1}_{A}(x)=G(x/A)
.

Note that, by definition, we always have
G(d/B)\leqslant G(d/A)
for all
d\in M
. Furthermore:

Proposition 2.9
.

For all
d\in M
, if
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, then
H(d/B)\geqslant H(d/A)
.

Proof.

Suppose
H(d/B)<H(d/A)
. Then there exists
a\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)
such that
a\not\in\textup{Stab}(d/B)
. Thus we can find
b\in B
which lies strictly between
d
and
d+a
.
d+a
d
b

on one hand,
b-a
lies strictly between
d
and
d-a
.
d-a
d+a
d
b-a
b

As
\textup{Stab}(d/A)
is a group, we have on the other hand
-a\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)
, i.e.
\textup{ct}(d+a/A)=\textup{ct}(d/A)=\textup{ct}(d-a/A)
. In particular, no point from
A
lies in the closed interval with bounds
b
and
b-a
. On the other hand,
d
lies strictly between
b
and
b-a
, which implies
d\underset{A}{{\not\downfree}^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
.∎

In the cut-independent setting, one may intuitively see
G(c/A)
as a distance that approximates the position of
c
“from the top”, while
H(c/A)
approximates
c
“from the bottom”. Then, when we go to a larger parameter set
B
, we get a finer approximation.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
a\in A
, and
d\in M
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

d-a\in G(d/A)
and
d\not\in A
.

•

\Delta(d-a)\not\in\Delta(A)
.

Proof.

Suppose
d-a\in G(d/A)
. If we had
\Delta(d-a)\in\Delta(A)
, then there would be
a^{\prime}\in A
such that
\Delta(d-a)=\Delta(a^{\prime})
. This would imply
a^{\prime}\in(G(d/A)\cap A)=\textup{Stab}(d/A)
. Since, for some
n
,
d-n|a^{\prime}|\leqslant a\leqslant d+n|a^{\prime}|
, as
na^{\prime}\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)
, it follows that
\textup{ct}(d/A)=\textup{ct}(a/A)
, thus
d=a
, proving the first direction.

Conversely, suppose
\Delta(d-a)\not\in\Delta(A)
. By definition of
G(d/A)
, in order to show that
a\in(d\textup{ mod }G(d/A))
, it suffices to show that
a\in\left]d-|a^{\prime}|,d+|a^{\prime}|\right[
(i.e.
|d-a|\leqslant|a^{\prime}|
) for every
a^{\prime}\in A\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/A)
. Suppose by contradiction this fails for some
a^{\prime}
. Then
\Delta(d-a)\geqslant\Delta(a^{\prime})
, and this inequality is strict by the hypothesis. As
a^{\prime}\not\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)
, let
a^{\prime\prime}\in A
which lies strictly between
d
and
d+a^{\prime}
.
d
a^{\prime\prime}
d+a^{\prime}
0
a^{\prime\prime}-d
a^{\prime}

Then
|d-a^{\prime\prime}|\leqslant|a^{\prime}|
, thus
\Delta(d-a^{\prime\prime})<\Delta(d-a)
. We then apply
remark

1.20
to get
\Delta(d-a)=\Delta(d-a^{\prime\prime}-d+a)=\Delta(a-a^{\prime\prime})\in\Delta(A)
, a contradiction.
∎

Definition 2.11
.

If
a
satisfies the conditions of
proposition

2.10
, then we say that
a
is a
ramifier of
d
over
A
. We write
\textup{ram}(d/A)
for the set of those ramifiers, and we say that
d
is
ramified over
A
if this set is non-empty. We say that
d
is
Archimedean over
A
whenever it is not ramified over
A
.

In
example

1.30
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are Archimedean over
A
,
c_{3}
is ramified over
A
, and
0
is a ramifier.

Remark 2.12
.

If
d\in M
, then
d
is ramified over
A
if and only if it satisfies the following equivalent conditions:

(1)

The coset
d\textup{ mod }G(d/A)
has a point in
A
, and
d\not\in A
.

(2)

\Delta(A+\mathbb{Q}d)\supsetneq\Delta(A)
.

Moreover, as
A\cap G(d/A)=\textup{Stab}(d/A)
,
\textup{ram}(d/A)
is a coset from the quotient
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\textup{Stab}(d/A)}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\textup{Stab}(d/A)}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\textup{Stab}(d/A)}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\textup{Stab}(d/A)}$}}}
, thus all its elements lie in the same coset
\textup{ mod }H(d/A)
. It turns out that
d
does not belong to that coset:

Lemma 2.13
.

Let
d\in M\setminus A
and
a\in A
. Then
d-a\not\in H(d/A)
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction that there exists
a^{\prime}\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)
such that
|d-a|\leqslant|a^{\prime}|
. Then
a\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
, thus
\Delta(d-a)\not\in\Delta(A)
, and in particular
\Delta(d-a)<\Delta(a^{\prime})
. By
remark

1.20
,
\Delta(d-(a+a^{\prime}))=\Delta(a^{\prime})\in\Delta(A)
, thus
a+a^{\prime}\not\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
. This contradicts the fact that
a\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
, and the fact that
a^{\prime}\in\textup{Stab}(d/A)
.
∎

Corollary 2.14
.

Let
d\in M
be ramified over
A
, as well as two elements
a
and
a^{\prime}
in
\textup{ram}(d/A)
. Then
\Delta(d-a)=\Delta(d-a^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We have
d-a\not\in H(d/A)\ni a-a^{\prime}
, thus
\Delta(d-a)>\Delta(a-a^{\prime})
, and by
remark

1.20
we have
\Delta(d-a)=\Delta(d-a+a-a^{\prime})=\Delta(d-a^{\prime})
.
∎

Definition 2.15
.

Let
d\in M
be ramified over
A
. We define
\delta(d/A)
as
\Delta(d-a)
for some
a\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
. This definition does not depend on the choice of the ramifier
a
by
corollary

2.14
.

Note that
\delta(d/A)
is the unique element of
\Delta(A+\mathbb{Q}\cdot d)\setminus\Delta(A)
.

Remark 2.16
.

Let
d
be ramified over
A
, and
a\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
. Then, as
G(d/A)
and
H(d/A)
have the same points in
A
, one can easily compute
\textup{ct}(\delta/\Delta(A))
. A convex subgroup is induced by its Archimedean classes, so by definition of the
\vee
-definable convex subgroup
H=H(d/A)
, for any model
N
containing
A
,
\Delta(H(N))
is the least initial segment of
\Delta(N)
containing
\Delta(\textup{Stab}(d/A))
. Now, as
\delta\in\Delta(G(d/A))\setminus\Delta(H)
, we clearly have
\textup{ct}(\delta/\Delta(A))=\textup{ct}_{>}(\Delta(\textup{Stab}(d/A))/\Delta(A))
.

\Delta(0)
\delta(d/A)
\Delta(M)
\Delta(H(d/A))
\Delta(G(d/A))

M
a
d
a\textup{ mod }H(d/A)
a\textup{ mod }G(d/A)

Many notions that we manipulate behave very differently with ramified and Archimedean points, it will often lead to case disjunctions. For instance, the following statements show us that cuts do not look the same for Archimedean and ramified points:

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
d\in M
. Then
\textup{ct}(d/A)\subseteq(d\textup{ mod }G(d/A))
(as type-definable sets).

Proof.

By contraposition, let
d^{\prime}\in M
be such that
d-d^{\prime}\not\in G(d/A)
. Then, by definition, there exists
a\in A\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/A)
such that
|d-d^{\prime}|\geqslant|a|
. Let
a^{\prime}\in A
which lies strictly between
d
and
d+a
. As either
d+a
or
d-a
lies strictly between
d
and
d^{\prime}
, either
a^{\prime}
or
a^{\prime}-a
lies strictly between
d
and
d^{\prime}
, thus
\textup{ct}(d/A)\neq\textup{ct}(d^{\prime}/A)
.
∎

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
d\in M
be Archimedean over
A
. Then the
A
-type-definable set
\textup{ct}(d/A)
coincides with
d\textup{ mod }G(d/A)
.

Proof.

Suppose
d\not\in A
, else the proposition is trivial.

We have the inclusion
d\textup{ mod }G(d/A)\subseteq\textup{ct}(d/A)
, as
d\textup{ mod }G(d/A)
is a convex set containing
d
and disjoint from
A
. The other direction follows from
lemma

2.17
.
∎

Proposition 2.19
.

Let
d\in M
be ramified over
A
. Then
\textup{ct}(d/A)
can be written as an
A
-translate of one of the two connected components of
G(d/A)\setminus H(d/A)
.

These translates are exactly the two blue segments in the last figure of
remark

2.16
. The connected components of some set will always refer to its maximal convex subsets.

Proof.

Let
a\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
. By
lemma

2.17
, we have:

\textup{ct}(d/A)\subseteq d\textup{ mod }G(d/A)=a\textup{ mod }G(d/A)

By
lemma

2.13
,
d\not\in(a\textup{ mod }H(d/A))
, which is the convex closure of the set
\textup{ram}(d/A)
, which is a subset of
A
, thus
a\textup{ mod }H(d/A)
must be disjoint from
\textup{ct}(d/A)
. It follows that
\textup{ct}(d/A)
is included in the connected component of
(a+G(d/A))\setminus(a+H(d/A))
containing
d
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be that connected component, and
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. There does not exist
a^{\prime}\in A
lying between
d
and
d^{\prime}
, for such
a^{\prime}
would be in
(a+G(d/A))\cap A=\textup{ram}(d/A)\subseteq a+H(d/A)
, and
a^{\prime}
would also belong to
\mathcal{C}
as
\mathcal{C}
is convex, contradicting the fact that
\mathcal{C}
is disjoint from
a+H(d/A)
.
∎

Corollary 2.20
.

With the hypothesis from
proposition

2.19
, if
a
is in
\textup{ram}(d/A)
, and
d^{\prime}
is another point from
M
, then
d^{\prime}\equiv_{A}d
if and only if the following conditions hold:

•

\textup{ct}(\Delta(d^{\prime}-a)/\Delta(A))=\textup{ct}(\Delta(d-a)/\Delta(A))
(hence
\Delta(d^{\prime}-a)
is not in
\Delta(A)
).

•

d^{\prime}<a\Longleftrightarrow d<a
.

Proof.

The first condition is equivalent to
d^{\prime}-a\in G(d/A)\setminus H(d/A)
. The second condition ensures that
d^{\prime}
lies in the correct connected component.
∎

The two statements
proposition

2.18
and
proposition

2.19
yield a simple classification of the cuts over
A
. In order to consider non-forking extensions of types, we now have to deal with ways to refine those cuts to cuts over a larger parameter set
B
. As we classified the cuts over
A
by looking at
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant convex subgroups, we have to compute what are the
\textup{Aut}(M/B)
-invariant convex subgroup corresponding to their refinements.

Definition 2.21
.

If
G
is an
A
-type-definable convex subgroup, then we define the following
A
-
\vee
-definable convex subgroup:

\underline{G}_{A}=\bigcup\limits_{a\in A\cap G}\left[-|a|,|a|\right]

If
H
is an
A
-
\vee
-definable convex subgroup, then we define the following
A
-type-definable convex subgroup:

\overline{H}^{A}=\bigcap\limits_{a\in A\setminus H}\left]-|a|,|a|\right[

Remark 2.22
.

Let
d\in M
. We have:

H(d/A)\leqslant{\overline{H(d/A)}^{M}}\leqslant{\overline{H(d/A)}^{B}}\leqslant{\underline{G(d/A)}_{B}}\leqslant{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}\leqslant G(d/A)

and :

\underline{G(d/A)}_{A}=H(d/A),\ {\overline{H(d/A)}^{A}}=G(d/A)

moreover, if
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, then by
proposition

2.9
we recall:

H(d/A)\leqslant H(d/B)\leqslant G(d/B)\leqslant G(d/A).

2.2.
Weak orthogonality

Now we start to deal with global invariant extensions, in order to prove
(C7)
.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
d\in M\setminus A
be Archimedean over
A
, with
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
. Let
p
be a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d/B)
. Then
G(d/B)=G(d/A)
(as
B
-type-definable sets), and:

p(x)\models x-d\in G(d/A)\setminus{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}

Note that the statement holds even if the element
d
is ramified over
B
.

Proof.

Suppose by contradiction
G(d/B)<G(d/A)
. Then there exists
b
in
B\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/B)
such that
b\in G(d/A)
. Let
b_{1}\in B
which lies strictly between
d
and
d+b
. Like wise, as
-b
is also in
G(d/A)\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/B)
, we find
b_{2}\in B
which lies strictly between
d
and
d-b
. Then the closed interval with bounds
b_{1},b_{2}
contains
d
and is strictly included in
d\textup{ mod }G(d/A)=\textup{ct}(d/A)
, which contradicts cut-independence.

By
proposition

2.18
, as
p
extends
\textup{tp}(d/A)
, we necessarily have:

p(x)\models x-d\in G(d/A)

Let
c\models p
in some elementary extension. As
p
is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant, we have
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}M
, thus
G(c/M)=G(d/A)
by the above paragraph (
c
is of course Archimedean over
A
). As
c-d\in G(c/M)
, and
d\in M\not\ni c
, we have
d\in\textup{ram}(c/M)
, thus
c-d\not\in H(c/M)={\underline{G(c/M)}_{M}}={\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}
. We conclude that
p(x)\models x-d\not\in{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
d\in M
be ramified over
A
, with
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, and let
a\in\textup{ram}(d/A)
. Then
\Delta(d-a)\not\in\Delta(B)
.

Moreover, if
p
is a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d/B)
, then, either (
p(x)\models x-a\in G(d/A)\setminus{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}
and
G(d/B)=G(d/A)
) or (
p(x)\models x-a\in{\overline{H(d/A)}^{M}}\setminus H(d/A)
and
H(d/B)=H(d/A)
).

Proof.

Suppose we have
\Delta(d-a)=\Delta(b)
for some
b\in B
. Let
n>0
such that
\dfrac{1}{n}|b|\leqslant|d-a|\leqslant n|b|
. Then we have
d\in\left[a-n|b|,a-\dfrac{1}{n}|b|\right]\cup\left[a+\dfrac{1}{n}b,a+n|b|\right]
. As
b\in G(d/A)\setminus H(d/A)
, none of those two intervals has a point in
A
, which contradicts
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
.

As
d
is ramified over
A
, and
p
extends
\textup{tp}(d/A)
, it follows from
proposition

2.19
that
p(x)\models x-a\in G(d/A)\setminus H(d/A)
.

Suppose by contradiction
p(x)\models x-a\in{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}\setminus{\overline{H(d/A)}^{M}}
. Then there exists
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
such that
m_{1}\not\in H(d/A)
,
m_{2}\in G(d/A)
, and:

p(x)\models|m_{1}|\leqslant|x-a|\leqslant|m_{2}|

i.e.
p(x)\models x\in\left[a-|m_{2}|,a-|m_{1}|\right]\cup\left[a+|m_{1}|,a+|m_{2}|\right]
. As
m_{1}\not\in H(d/A)
and
m_{2}\in G(d/A)
, each of those two intervals is included in each connected component of
a+(G(d/A)\setminus H(d/A))
. As a result, none of those intervals belongs to
p
by
corollary

1.8
, a contradiction.

By
lemma

2.23
, and by
remark

2.22
, if we had
p(x)\models x-a\in{\underline{G(d/A)}_{B}}
(resp.
p(x)\models x-a\not\in{\overline{H(d/A)}^{B}}
), then we would have:

p(x)\models x-a\in{\overline{H(d/A)}^{M}}\setminus H(d/A)

and respectively:
p(x)\models x-a\in G(d/A)\setminus{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}
. To conclude, it now suffices to show the two following properties:

(1)

If
G(d/B)<G(d/A)
, then
\textup{tp}(d/B)\models x-a\in{\underline{G(d/A)}_{B}}
.

(2)

If
H(d/B)>H(d/A)
, then
\textup{tp}(d/B)\models x-a\not\in{\overline{H(d/A)}^{B}}
.

Let us proceed:

(1)

By hypothesis, let
b\in B\setminus\textup{Stab}(d/B)
such that
b\in G(d/A)
. Let
b^{\prime}\in B
which lies strictly between
d
and
d+b
.

a
b^{\prime}-b
d
b^{\prime}
d+b

then the interval
I
with bounds
b^{\prime}-a
,
b^{\prime}-b-a
is included in
G(d/A)
, and
\textup{tp}(d/B)\models x-a\in I
.

0
b^{\prime}-b-a
d-a
b^{\prime}-a
d+b-a

(2)

By hypothesis, let
b\in\textup{Stab}(d/B)
such that
b\not\in H(d/A)
. Then we have
\textup{ct}(d-b/B)=\textup{ct}(d/B)=\textup{ct}(d+b/B)
, which implies
a\not\in\left[d-|b|,d+|b|\right]
. As a result,
\textup{tp}(d/B)\models x-a\not\in\left[-|b|,|b|\right]
.∎

The two statements
lemma

2.23
,
lemma

2.24
imply:

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
d\in M
such that
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
. Let
p\in S(M)
be a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d/B)
. Then there exists
f
an
A
-definable map such that one of the three following conditions hold:

•

d\in A
,
f=id
.

•

The cut over
M
induced by
p
is one of the two connected components of the translation by
f(d)
of
G(d/A)\setminus{\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}}
. In that case,
G(d/B)=G(d/A)
.

•

The cut over
M
induced by
p
is one of the two connected components of the translation by
f(d)
of
{\overline{H(d/A)}^{M}}\setminus H(d/A)
. In that case,
H(d/B)=H(d/A)
.

Proof.

If
d\in A
, then the statement is trivial. If
d\not\in A
and
d
is Archimedean over
A
, then the second condition holds with
f=id
, as stated in
lemma

2.23
. If
d
is ramified over
A
, then either the second or the third condition holds with
f:x\longmapsto x-a
, by
lemma

2.24
.
∎

Remark 2.26
.

If
d\not\in A
, while only one of the conditions from
corollary

2.25
holds, we might still have
G(d/B)=G(d/A)
and
H(d/B)=H(d/A)
at the same time. For instance, this is the case whenever
\Delta(d)<\Delta(B\setminus\{0\})
, and
B
has no element that is infinitesimal compared to
A
.

In fact, one can see that
p
is the type of an
M
-ramified point which adds an Archimedean class
\delta
which is cut-independent over
\Delta(A)
: the two conditions of the statement expresses that
\delta
either leans left or right with respect to
\Delta(A)
and
\Delta(M)
.

Remark 2.27
.

For all
d\in M
, recall from
remark

2.22
that
G(d/B)={\overline{H(d/B)}^{B}}
. As a result, if
H(d/B)=H(d/A)
, then
G(d/B)={\overline{H(d/A)}^{B}}
. In particular we see that, in either case of
corollary

2.25
,
p(x)
implies
x-f(d)\in G(d/B)
. Likewise:

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
d\in M
such that
d\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, and
d\not\in A
. Let
p
be a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(d/B)
. Then
p(x)
implies
x\not\in(M+H(d/B))
.

Proof.

As
d\not\in A
, the first condition of
corollary

2.25
fails. Let
f
be the
A
-definable map witnessing
corollary

2.25
.

As
G(d/A)\geqslant G(d/B)
, we have:

\underline{G(d/A)}_{M}\geqslant\underline{G(d/B)}_{M}\geqslant H(d/B)

If the second condition holds, let
G=G(d/B),H=\underline{G(d/B)}_{M}
, else let
G=\overline{H(d/B)}^{M},H=H(d/B)
. Either way,
G
and
H
have the same points in
M
,
p(x)\models x-f(d)\in G\setminus H
, and
H\geqslant H(d/B)
. Suppose by contradiction that there is
m\in M
such that
p(x)\models x-m\in H(d/B)
. Then, as
p(x)\models x-f(d)\in G
, we have
m-f(d)\in G
, yet
m-f(d)\in M
, therefore
m-f(d)\in H
. This is a contradiction, as we cannot have at the same time
p(x)\models x-f(d)\not\in H
,
p(x)\models x-m\in H
and
f(d)-m\in H
.
∎

We established that realizations of unary global invariant types belong to some cosets of convex subgroups, and not others. Just as in
lemma

1.29
, we may relate those statements to orthogonality, in order to prove
(C7)
.

Lemma 2.29
.

Let
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
be a family of tuples of
M
such that, for all
i
,
\textup{tp}(d_{i}/A)
and
\textup{tp}(d_{<i}/A)
are weakly orthogonal. Then the family of types
(\textup{tp}(d_{1}/A),\ldots,\textup{tp}(d_{n}/A))
is weakly orthogonal with respect to
definition

1.28
.

Proof.

We show by induction on
i
that
(\textup{tp}(d_{1}/A),\ldots,\textup{tp}(d_{i}/A))
is weakly orthogonal. The statement is trivial if
i=1
. By induction hypothesis, we have
\bigcup\limits_{j<i}\textup{tp}(d_{j}/A)\models\textup{tp}(d_{<i}/A)
. By hypothesis,
\textup{tp}(d_{i}/A)\cup\textup{tp}(d_{<i}/A)\models\textup{tp}(d_{\leqslant i}/A)
, and we conclude by induction.
∎

Lemma 2.30
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2}
be two tuples from
M
. Assume for all
f
in
\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
that
\textup{tp}(f(d_{1})/A)
is weakly orthogonal to
\textup{tp}(d_{2}/A)
. Then
\textup{tp}(d_{1}/A)
is weakly orthogonal to
\textup{tp}(d_{2}/A)
.

Proof.

Let
d\equiv_{A}d_{1}
. It suffices to show that
d\equiv_{Ad_{2}}d_{1}
. By quantifier elimination, this holds if and only if, for every
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
, we have
f(d)\equiv_{Ad_{2}}f(d_{1})
, which is exactly the hypothesis of the statement.
∎

Lemma 2.31
.

Let
d_{1}\in M
be a singleton, and let
d_{2}\in M
be a tuple. Let
V
be the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\textup{dcl}(Ad_{2})
, and let
G
be some
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant convex subgroup of
M
. Suppose we have
V\subseteq(A+G)\not\ni d_{1}
. Then
\textup{tp}(d_{1}/A)
and
\textup{tp}(d_{2}/A)
are weakly orthogonal.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of
lemma

1.29
.
∎

Let us now prove
(C7)
:

Proposition 2.32
.

Let
c=(c_{ij})_{ij}
be a finite tuple from
M
that is
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-separated, such that its
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-blocks are the
(c_{i})_{i}=((c_{ij})_{j})_{i}
. Suppose we have
(p_{i}(x_{i}))_{i}
a weak
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-block extension of the
c_{ij}
. Then
(p_{i})_{i}
is weakly orthogonal.

Recall from
definition

1.34
that by definition, types in a block extension are
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant. In particular, we have
c_{i}\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
for each
i
.

Proof.

Enumerate the
c_{i}
in increasing order of their values. Let
N
be some
|M|^{+}
-saturated, strongly
|M|^{+}
-homogeneous elementary extension of
M
, and let
d_{i}\in N
be a realization of
p_{i}
. By
lemma

2.29
, and
lemma

2.30
, it suffices to show that for all
i
, for all
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
,
\textup{tp}(f(c_{i})/M)
is weakly orthogonal to
\textup{tp}(c_{<i}/M)
. This is trivial if
f=0
.

Suppose
f\neq 0
. By
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-separatedness,
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}(f(c_{i}))=\textup{val}^{1}_{B}(c_{i})
. Let
H=H(f(c_{i})/B)
. As
H
is a
B
-
\vee
-definable group,
H(N)
is
\textup{Aut}(M/N)
-invariant. As
\textup{tp}(f(d_{i})/M)
is a global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extension of
\textup{tp}(f(c_{i})/M)
, we have
f(d_{i})\not\in(M+H(N))
by
lemma

2.28
. Let
V=\textup{dcl}(Md_{<i})
. By
lemma

2.31
, it suffices to show that
V\subseteq(M+H(N))
. Let
v\in V
. Let
g\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
and
m\in M
be such that
v=m+g(d_{<i})
. Let
h
be the
A
-definable map witnessing
corollary

2.25
applied to
g(d_{<i})
. As
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
is a
B
-valuation, and the values of the blocks before
c_{i}
are strictly smaller than that of
c_{i}
, we have
G(g(c_{<i})/B)<G(c_{i}/B)
, thus
G(g(c_{<i})/B)<H
by
proposition

2.6
. By
remark

2.27
:

g(d_{<i})-h\circ g(c_{<i})\in G(g(c_{<i})/B)<H

It follows that
v=m+h\circ g(c_{<i})+(g(d_{<i})-h\circ g(c_{<i}))\in(M+H(N))
, concluding the proof.
∎

Let us now build
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
:

Lemma 2.33
.

Let
d,d^{\prime}\in M
be such that
G(d/A)=G(d^{\prime}/A)
,
d
is Archimedean over
A
and
d^{\prime}
is ramified over
A
. Then
G(d+d^{\prime}/A)=G(d/A)
and
d+d^{\prime}
is Archimedean over
A
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\textup{ram}(d^{\prime}/A)
, and let
G=G(d/A)=G(d^{\prime}/A)
. Then the (convex) coset
d+a\textup{ mod }G
has no point in
A
and contains
d+d^{\prime}
, in particular
d+d^{\prime}\textup{ mod }G\subseteq\textup{ct}(d+d^{\prime}/A)
. By ultrametric inequality for the valuation
v^{1}_{A}
,
G(d+d^{\prime}/A)\leqslant G
. By
lemma

2.17
,
\textup{ct}(d+d^{\prime}/A)
is contained in
d+d^{\prime}\textup{ mod }G(d+d^{\prime}/A)
, therefore we have:

d+d^{\prime}\textup{ mod }G\subseteq\textup{ct}(d+d^{\prime}/A)\subseteq d+d^{\prime}\textup{ mod }G(d+d^{\prime}/A)\subseteq d+d^{\prime}\textup{ mod }G

As a result,
G(d+d^{\prime}/A)=G
. This concludes the proof, as the coset
d+d^{\prime}\textup{ mod }G(d+d^{\prime}/A)
has no point in
A
.
∎

Corollary 2.34
.

The following satisfies the conditions of
lemma

1.21
:

x\leqslant y

\Longleftrightarrow

either
\textup{val}^{1}_{A}(x)<\textup{val}^{1}_{A}(y)
, or they have the same value and
x
is ramified over
A
, or they have the same value and
y
is Archimedean over
A
.

Definition 2.35
.

We define
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
the valuation induced by the above preorder. It is clearly an
A
-valuation which refines
\textup{val}^{1}_{A}
.

This new valuation splits the
\textup{val}^{1}_{A}
-blocks in two, putting the
A
-ramified points below the
A
-Archimedean ones. For instance, in
example

1.30
, we have
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(c_{1})<\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(c_{3})<\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(c_{2})
.

Let us now prove
(C6)
:

Proposition 2.36
.

Let
c=(c_{ij})_{ij}
be a finite tuple from
M
that is
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
-separated, such that its
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
-blocks are the
(c_{i})_{i}=((c_{ij})_{j})_{i}
. Then
(\textup{tp}(c_{i}/A))_{i}
is weakly orthogonal.

Note that the types which are weakly orthogonal are types over
A
here, as opposed to the types in
proposition

2.32
which were global types. The respective restrictions over
A
of non-weakly orthogonal global types can very well be weakly orthogonal.

Proof.

Just like in
proposition

2.32
, enumerate the blocks in increasing order of their value by
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
. Choose
i
, and
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
with
f\neq 0
. As in the proof of
proposition

2.32
, it suffices to find
G
an
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant convex subgroup of
M
such that
f(c_{i})\not\in(A+G)
, and, for all
g\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
,
g(c_{<i})\in(A+G)
.

•

Suppose the
(c_{ij})_{j}
are ramified over
A
, then we choose
G=H(c_{i}/A)
. By separatedness,
G=H(f(c_{i})/A)
. Now, for any
g\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
, we have by
proposition

2.6

G(g(c_{<i})/A)<G
, therefore there exists
a
in
A\setminus\textup{Stab}(g(c_{<i})/A)
such that
a\in G
. Let
a^{\prime}\in A
be such that
a^{\prime}\in\left]g(c_{<i})-|a|,g(c_{<i})+|a|\right[
. Then
g(c_{<i})\in\left]a^{\prime}-|a|,a^{\prime}+|a|\right[\subseteq(A+G)
.

•

Suppose the
(c_{ij})_{j}
are Archimedean over
A
, and choose
g
. This time we let
G=G(c_{i}/A)
. By definition of being Archimedean,
f(c_{i})\not\in(A+G)
. Either
G(g(c_{<i})/A)<G
, in which case we have
g(c_{<i})\in(A+H(c_{i}/A))\subseteq(A+G)
just as in the above item, or
G(g(c_{<i})/A)=G
, in which case
g(c_{<i})
is ramified over
A
as
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(g(c_{<i}))<\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(c_{i})
, therefore
g(c_{<i})\in(A+G)
.

This concludes the proof.
∎

2.3.
Tensor product

We saw that the blocks of a block extension for the coarsest valuation are weakly orthogonal, thus can be glued by just taking the union. It will no longer be the case for finer valuations, the tensor product of the blocks will no longer commute, and we will have to choose the order of the factors. For the third valuation, which is yet undefined, this choice will have to be taken with care, because some choices may give a global type which does not extend our type over
B
.

The following proposition shows us how to properly glue the elements of a
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
-block extension.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
c
be a finite tuple from
M
such that
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
. Suppose
c=(c_{i})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
is a
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
-block (i.e. a family having only one block) that is
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
-separated, and
c_{1}
(resp.
c_{2}
) is the block of
B
-ramified (resp. Archimedean) elements of
c
. Suppose
p_{1},p_{2}
is a weak
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
-block extension of
c
. Then
c
satisfies the restriction to
B
of the tensor product of
p_{1},p_{2}
in any order.

Note that, although the restrictions to
B
of
p_{1}\otimes p_{2}
and
p_{2}\otimes p_{1}
coincide, these two global types are distinct, i.e.
(p_{1},p_{2})
is not weakly orthogonal. This will be explained in the next section.

Proof.

By
proposition

2.36
,
(\textup{tp}(c_{i}/B))_{i\in\{1,2\}}
is weakly orthogonal, thus
\textup{tp}(c_{1}/B)
is complete in
S(B+\mathbb{Q}c_{2})
, and it coincides in particular with
(p_{1})_{|B+\mathbb{Q}c_{2}}
. It follows that
c\models(p_{1}\otimes p_{2})_{|B}
, and the same reasoning can be applied to
p_{2}\otimes p_{1}
.
∎

Let us now build
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
:

Lemma 2.38
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2}\in M
be two points which are ramified over
A
, and have the same
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
-value. Suppose
\delta(d_{1}/A)<\delta(d_{2}/A)
. Then
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(d_{1}+d_{2})=\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(d_{2})
, and
\delta(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)=\delta(d_{2}/A)
(as defined in
definition

2.15
).

Proof.

Let
a_{i}\in\textup{ram}(d_{i}/A)
. Then, by
remark

1.20
,
\Delta(d_{1}+d_{2}-a_{1}-a_{2})=\delta(d_{2}/A)
, therefore
d_{1}+d_{2}
is ramified over
A
, and
\delta(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)=\delta(d_{2}/A)
. By
remark

2.16
, we have:

\textup{ct}_{>}(\Delta(\textup{Stab}(d_{2}/A))/\Delta(A))=\textup{ct}_{>}(\Delta(\textup{Stab}(d_{1}+d_{2}/A))/\Delta(A))

However, those stabilizers are convex subgroups of
A
, thus the sets of their Archimedean classes are initial segments of
\Delta(A)
, so this equality of cuts implies
\Delta(\textup{Stab}(d_{2}/A))=\Delta(\textup{Stab}(d_{1}+d_{2}/A))
. It follows that
\textup{Stab}(d_{2}/A)=\textup{Stab}(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)
, thus
H(d_{2}/A)=H(d_{1}+d_{2}/A)
, thus
\textup{val}^{1}_{A}(d_{2})=\textup{val}^{1}_{A}(d_{1}+d_{2})
. This concludes the proof, as
d_{2}
,
d_{1}+d_{2}
are both ramified over
A
.
∎

Corollary 2.39
.

The following satisfies the conditions of
lemma

1.21
:

x\leqslant y

\Longleftrightarrow

either
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(x)<\textup{val}^{2}_{A}(y)
, or they have the same value, they are both ramified over
A
, and
\delta(x/A)\leqslant\delta(y/A)
.

Definition 2.40
.

We define
\textup{val}^{3}_{A}
the valuation induced by the above preorder. It is clearly an
A
-valuation which refines
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
.

The valuation splits the ramified
\textup{val}^{2}_{A}
-blocks in a way which depends on the Archimedean classes
\delta
added by their elements. Note that, while for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\textup{val}^{i}_{A}
factors through the quotient by
A
-elementary equivalence (these are model-theoretic objects),
\textup{val}^{3}_{A}
is an
algebraic
object that is no longer refined by types.

We need to define additional notions in order to better understand
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-block extensions. Contrary to
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
and
\textup{val}^{1}_{B}
, a weak
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-block extension of some tuple might not be strong. We need to find necessary and sufficient conditions for such a block extension to be strong.

Proposition 2.41
.

Let
c=(c_{ij})_{ij}
be a finite
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
-block of
B
-ramified points from
M
that is
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-separated, and whose
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-blocks are the
(c_{i})_{i}=((c_{ij})_{j})_{i}
. Suppose the indices
i
are ordered such that:
i<k\Longleftrightarrow\delta(c_{i}/B)<\delta(c_{k}/B)
. Let
d=(d_{ij})_{ij}
be another tuple from
M
. Then
c\equiv_{B}d
if and only if the following conditions hold:

(1)

For each
i
, we have
d_{i}\equiv_{B}c_{i}
. In particular, each
d_{i}
is a
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-block of
B
-ramified points that has the same
\textup{val}^{2}_{B}
-value as
c
.

(2)

For every
i
and
k
,
i<k
if and only if
\delta(d_{i}/B)<\delta(d_{k}/B)
.

Proof.

For each
i
and
j
, let
b_{ij}\in\textup{ram}(c_{ij}/B)
.

Suppose
c\equiv_{B}d
(then condition
1
holds). Let
i,k,j,l
be indices. If
i<k
, then
\textup{tp}(c/B)\models|x_{ij}-b_{ij}|<\dfrac{1}{n}|x_{kl}-b_{kl}|
for every
n
, therefore we have
\Delta(d_{ij}-b_{ij})<\Delta(d_{kl}-b_{kl})
. Moreover, by
corollary

2.20
, we have
\delta(d_{ij}/B)=\Delta(d_{ij}-b_{ij})
(and similarly for
kl
), and condition
2
holds.

Conversely, suppose that the conditions of the list hold. Let
f_{i}
be in
\textup{LC}^{|c_{i}|}(\mathbb{Q})
, and let
b^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{i}f_{i}(b_{i})
. We have to prove that
c^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{i}f_{i}(c_{i})
and
d^{\prime}=\sum\limits_{i}f_{i}(d_{i})
have the same cut over
B
. This is trivial if each
f_{i}
is zero, else let
k
be the maximal index for which
f_{k}
is non-zero. By
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}
-separatedness,
\textup{val}^{3}_{B}(f_{k}(c_{k}))=\textup{val}^{3}_{B}(c_{kj})
for any
j
. Moreover,
f_{k}((c_{kj}-b_{kj})_{j})
is a linear combination of elements of
G(c_{k}/B)
, hence it belongs to
G(c_{k}/B)
, and
f_{k}(b_{k})
is a ramifier of
f_{k}(c_{k})
. Likewise, we have
f_{i}(c_{i})-f_{i}(b_{i})\in G(c_{i}/B)
for any
i
. It follows by maximality of
k
that:

(3)

\delta(c_{k}/B)=\Delta(f_{k}(c_{k})-f_{k}(b_{k}))>\Delta(f_{i}(c_{i})-f_{i}(b_{i}))

for each
i\neq k
. By the conditions of the list, this also holds for
d
. Choose an arbitrary index
j
. There exists
n\in\omega_{>0}
and
\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
f_{k}(c_{k})-f_{k}(b_{k})
lies between
\dfrac{\varepsilon}{n}(c_{kj}-b_{kj})
and
\varepsilon n(c_{kj}-b_{kj})
. By the first condition, this must hold for
c
replaced with
d
(with the same
\varepsilon,n
). Moreover, as
3
holds for
d
, for all
i\neq k
and
N>0
, we have
|f_{i}(d_{i})-f_{i}(b_{i})|<\dfrac{1}{N}|d_{kj}-b_{kj}|
. By choosing
N
large enough, and by summing everything, we get that
d^{\prime}-b^{\prime}
lies between
\dfrac{\varepsilon}{N+1}(d_{kj}-b_{kj})
and
\varepsilon(N+1)(d_{kj}-b_{kj})
, and we get the same property with
d
replaced by
c
. Then we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textup{ct}(\Delta(c^{\prime}-b^{\prime})/\Delta(B))&=&\textup{ct}(\Delta(c_{kj}-b_{kj})/\Delta(B))\\
&=&\textup{ct}(\Delta(d_{kj}-b_{kj})/\Delta(B))\\
&=&\textup{ct}(\Delta(d^{\prime}-b^{\prime})/\Delta(B))\end{array}

The second equality is a consequence of
corollary

2.20
applied to
c_{kj}
,
d_{kj}
(by the hypothesis
c_{k}\equiv_{B}d_{k}
