, and they are all surjective. We write
(\pi_{j})_{j}
for those maps.

Assumptions 5.2
.

We fix an ordered Abelian group
M
, and a special subgroup
A\leqslant M
, such that
{M}
is
|A|^{+}
-saturated and strongly
|A|^{+}
-homogeneous.

Lemma 5.3

(Standard variant of the Chinese remainder theorem)
.

Let
L
be a finite set of primes, and
N>0
. Then for all
(a_{l})_{l}\in M^{L}
, there exists
b\in M
such that
M\models\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(a_{l}-b)
for all
l\in L
.

Corollary 5.4
.

The natural map
S^{m}_{\textup{qf}}(A)\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{l\textup{ prime}}S^{m}_{l}(A)
is surjective.

Proof.

For each
l
, let
a_{l}=(a_{i,l})_{i}\in M^{m}
. By
lemma

5.3
and compactness, for each
i
, there must exist
b_{i}\in M
such that
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(b_{i}-a_{i,l})
for all
l
,
N
. In particular,
\textup{tp}_{l}(b/A)=\textup{tp}_{l}(a_{l}/A)
for all
l
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

The map
\pi:\ S^{m}_{\textup{qf}}(A)\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{j\in J}S^{m}_{j}(A)
is obviously injective. Note that
S^{m}_{<}(A)
is not a factor of the product of
lemma

5.3
, thus
\pi
may not be surjective.

6.
Basic properties of regular groups

The class of regular ordered Abelian groups (ROAG) has several equivalent definitions. The ones that are necessary for us are quantifier elimination in
\mathcal{L}_{P}
, and some compatibility conditions between the
<
-types and the
l
-types. The definitions involving Archimedean groups and definable convex subgroups will be relevant, because they give a motivation as to why we are interested in ROAG.

The equivalence between these different definitions is folklore. In this section, we prove the easy implications between these equivalent definitions, and we give references for the harder implications.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
G
be an ordered Abelian group. We say that
G
is
regular
if, for every positive integer
n
, every interval of
G
that contains at least
n
elements intersects
nG
.

For any ordered Abelian group
G
, we recall that
\textup{div}(G)
refers to the divisible closure of
G
, which we see as an ordered group to which the order on
G
naturally extends.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
G\models

ROAG
, and
A
a special subgroup of
G
. Let
F
be the closed subspace of
S^{m}_{\textup{qf}}(A)
of types of
m
-tuples that are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
(i.e. lifts in
G^{m}
of
\mathbb{Q}
-free families from
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\textup{div}(G)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\textup{div}(A)}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\textup{div}(G)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\textup{div}(A)}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\textup{div}(G)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\textup{div}(A)}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\textup{div}(G)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\textup{div}(A)}$}}}
). Then the map
F\longrightarrow\pi_{<}(F)\times\prod_{l\textup{ prime}}S^{m}_{l}(A)
is a homeomorphism.

In particular,
\pi_{l}(F)=S^{m}_{l}(A)
for every prime
l
.

Proof.

Two elements having the same type must have the same
j
-type for all
j\in J
, therefore the map is injective, and it is clearly continuous. Let us prove that it is surjective.

Let
p=(p_{j})_{j}
be in the direct product. Let
c=(c_{i})_{i}
be a realization of
p_{<}
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
. By
corollary

5.4
, let
c^{\prime}
be some tuple which realizes simultaneously all the
(p_{l})
for
l
prime (
c^{\prime}
might not be
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
, but it does not matter). Let us show that there exist
d=(d_{i})_{i<m}
such that
d_{i}-c^{\prime}_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N>0}NG
for all
i
, and
d\models p_{<}
(in particular,
d
will be
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
). The existence of
d
will be established by a disjunction of two cases.

Suppose
G
is discrete. Let
N>0
. Then, by regularity of
G
applied to the interval
[c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime}+N\cdot\text{1}]
, there must exist an integer
k_{i}
between
0
and
N
such that
c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime}+k_{i}\cdot\text{1}\in NG
. Let us show that
d^{\prime}=(d_{i}^{\prime})_{i}=(c_{i}+k_{i}\cdot\text{1})_{i}\models p_{<}
. If not, then there must exist some atomic formula with predicate
=
or
<
which is satisfied by
c
and not
d^{\prime}
, or vice-versa. Without loss, there must exist
a\in A
, and some non-zero
f\in\textup{LC}^{m}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
f(c)<a\leqslant f((c_{i}+k_{i}\cdot\text{1})_{i})
. By subtracting
f((k_{i}\cdot\text{1})_{i})
to the second inequality,
f(c)
belongs to the interval
[a-f((k_{i}\cdot\text{1})_{i}),a]
, which is included in
A
as
A
is special, contradicting
\mathbb{Q}
-freeness of
c
over
A
. It follows that
d^{\prime}\models p_{<}
, and we conclude by compactness that
d
exists.

Now, suppose
G
is dense. Let
D
be the special subgroup generated by
A
, and all the
(c_{i})_{i}
. For every positive element
a
of
D
, the open interval
]c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime}+a[
is infinite, thus contains an element of
NG
by regularity, for any
N>0
. By compactness, there exists, for each
i
, an element
d_{i}^{\prime}
such that
d_{i}^{\prime}\in]c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime},c_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime}+a[
for every positive
a\in D
, and
d^{\prime}_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N>0}NG
. Let
d_{i}=d_{i}^{\prime}+c_{i}^{\prime}
. Notice that
d_{i}-c_{i}
is a positive element which is infinitesimal with respect to
D
. It remains to show that
d\models p_{<}
. Let
f\in\textup{LC}^{m}(\mathbb{Z})
be non-zero, and
a\in A
, such that
f(c)>a
. As each
d_{i}-c_{i}
is infinitesimal with respect to
D
, this is also the case for
f(d-c)=f(d)-f(c)
, thus
|f(d)-f(c)|<f(c)-a
, thus
f(c)-f(d)<f(c)-f(a)
, and we conclude that
f(d)>f(a)
, proving that
d\models p_{<}
.

In both cases, we have that
d\models p_{<}
. As
d_{i}-c_{i}^{\prime}\in l^{N}G
for every
l
prime and
N>0
, we have
d\models p_{l}
for every
l
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

Note that, in this proof,
d_{i}
can always be chosen so that
d_{i}>c_{i}
for each
i
(in the discrete case, replace
k_{i}
by
k_{i}+N>0
). In particular, if we have
c=c^{\prime}
in the construction of the proof, then the tuple
d
that we build is distinct from
c
, and re-iterating this operation generates infinitely many pairwise-distinct tuples having the same quantifier-free type as
d
. It follows that any consistent quantifier-free type over
A
whose realizations are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
has infinitely many realizations.

Definition 6.4
.

Let
G
be an ordered Abelian group,
n<\omega
and
g\in G
. Define
H_{n}(g)
to be the largest convex subgroup of
G
for which we have
(g+H_{n}(g))\cap nG=\emptyset
(by convention, if
g\in nG
, then
H_{n}(g)=\{0\}
). This convex subgroup is definable:

H_{n}(g)=\{0\}\text{ or }\left\{x\in G|\forall y\in G,\ \left(|y|\leqslant n|x|\Longrightarrow g+y\not\in nG\right)\right\}

for if
x\in G
,
z\in G
satisfy
\Delta(x)=\Delta(z)
(
\Delta
is defined in
definition

1.24
) and
z+g\in nG
, then
x\not\in H_{n}(g)
is witnessed by choosing
y=z\pm mn|x|
, with
m
the least natural integer for which
|z|-mn|x|\leqslant n|x|
.

In the literature, the set of the
H_{n}(g)
for all
g\in G
is called the
n
-spine
of
G
. This is a definable family of definable convex subgroups. One of the most general “complexity class” of ordered Abelian groups that is still considered rather “nice” is the class of ordered Abelian groups with finite spines. This class contains in particular the dp-finite ordered Abelian groups. For reference, section 2 of
[
4
]
gives nice characterizations and a quantifier elimination result for this class.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
G
be an ordered Abelian group. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

G
does not have any proper non-trivial definable convex subgroup.

(2)

For all
g\in G
, for all
n<\omega

H_{n}(g)=\{0\}
.

(3)

G
is regular.

(4)

The theory of
G
eliminates quantifiers in the Presburger language.

(5)

There exists an Archimedean ordered Abelian group elementarily equivalent to
G
.

Proof.

The directions that we show in order to establish the equivalence are
1\Longrightarrow 2\Longrightarrow 3
,
3\Longrightarrow 4\Longrightarrow 1
and
3\Longrightarrow 5\Longrightarrow 1
.

The implications
1\Longrightarrow 2
and
5\Longrightarrow 1
are trivial,
3\Longrightarrow 4
is due to Weispfenning (
[
15
]
, Theorems 2.3 and 2.6), and Presburger in the discrete case, and
3\Longrightarrow 5
follows from (
[
16
]
, Theorem 2.5) and (
[
10
]
, Theorem 4.7).

Let us show
2\Longrightarrow 3
by contraposition. We have an interval
I
of
G
and an integer
n
such that
|I|\geqslant n
and
I\cap nG=\emptyset
. If
|I|<2n+1
, then
G
is discrete, and the points from
I+n\cdot\text{1}
have the same cosets
\textup{ mod }nG
as those of
I
, so we can assume
|I|\geqslant 2n+1
by replacing
I
by
I\cup(I+n\cdot\text{1})\cup(I+2n\cdot\text{1})
. Let
f
be a strictly increasing map
\{0,\ldots 2n\}\longrightarrow I
, and
h=\min\left\{f(i+1)-f(i)|0\leqslant i\leqslant 2n-1\right\}
. As
f
is strictly increasing, we have
h\neq 0
. As
h
is minimal, we can sum
n
many inequalities to get
n\cdot h\leqslant\sum\limits_{i=0}^{n-1}(f(i+1)-f(i))=f(n)-f(0)
. With the same argument,
n\cdot h\leqslant f(2n)-f(n)
, thus
\left[f(n)-n\cdot h,f(n)+n\cdot h\right]\subseteq I
, which is disjoint from
nG
. It follows that
h\in H_{n}(f(n))\setminus\{0\}
, so
2
fails.

Let us prove
4\Longrightarrow 1
by contraposition. Let
G
be an ordered Abelian group with a proper non-trivial definable convex subgroup
H
. Let
A
be a definably closed subset of
G
such that
H
is
A
-definable. Let
A_{1}=A_{\geqslant 0}\cap H
,
A_{2}=(A_{>0}\setminus H)\cup\{+\infty\}
. Let
X_{<}=\left(\bigcap\limits_{a\in A_{1},b\in A_{2}}]a,b[\right)
, an
A
-type definable set which is non-empty by compactness. For each prime
l
, let
X_{l}=\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G
, which corresponds to
\textup{tp}_{l}(0/A)
. Let us show that the partial type defined as
q(x):\ x\in X_{<}\cap\bigcap\limits_{l}X_{l}
is consistent with
H
and
\neg H
. Let
a\in A_{1}
,
b\in A_{2}
,
N>0
. By compactness, it suffices to show that
Y=]a,b[\cap NG
intersects
H
and
\neg H
, which is witnessed by
N\cdot a\in Y\cap H
, and
N\cdot(b-a)\in Y\setminus H
. Now let
h\in H,g\not\in H
be two realizations of
q
. Then
\textup{qftp}(h/A)=q=\textup{qftp}(g/A)
, but
\textup{tp}(h/A)\neq\textup{tp}(g/A)
, and the theory of
G
does not eliminate quantifiers in
\mathcal{L}_{P}
.∎

0
h
g
A_{1}
H
A_{2}

Note that in particular, the domain of the homeomorphism given by
lemma

6.2
is in fact a space of
complete types
.

Corollary 6.6
.

The definable closure of a parameter set
A
coincides with the special subgroup it generates.

Proof.

The special subgroup generated by
A
is clearly included in
\textup{dcl}(A)
. Conversely, if
A
is a special subgroup, and
c\not\in A
, then the
1
-tuple
c
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
, thus
c\not\in\textup{acl}(A)
by
remark

6.3
.
∎

There is another important corollary which allows us to better understand independence:

Corollary 6.7
.

Let
{M}\models
ROAG
, let
A\leqslant B
be special subgroups of
M
such that
{M}
is
|B|^{+}
saturated and strongly
|B|^{+}
-homogeneous, and let
c=c_{1}\ldots c_{n}\in M
. Suppose
c_{1}\ldots c_{n}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
, and let
F
be the closed subspace of
S^{n}(M)
of tuples that are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
M
. For each
j\in J
, consider the action of
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
on
\pi_{j}(F)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
(resp.
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}}B
).

(2)

For each
j\in J
,
\textup{tp}_{j}(c/B)
, which is a partial type over
B
, can be extended to an
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant (resp. of bounded orbit) element of
\pi_{j}(F)
.

Proof.

First of all, note that the global extensions of
\textup{tp}(c/B)
realized by tuples that are not
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
M
divide over
A
, which implies that they have an unbounded orbit (see for instance
[
14
]
, Exercise 7.1.5), so we do have to restrict ourselves to
F
.

For each
j\in J
,
F\longrightarrow\pi_{j}(F)
is an equivariant surjection, thus the stabilizer of a point of
F
is a subgroup of that of its image, so the cardinal of its orbit is larger than the supremum of the cardinals of the orbits of each of its images, and we get the top-to-bottom direction by contraposition.

Suppose for each
j
, we have
p_{j}\in\pi_{j}(F)
a witness of the second condition: it extends
\textup{tp}_{j}(c/B)
, and its orbit is a singleton (resp. bounded). Note that the orbit of
(p_{j})_{j}
under
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
is contained in the Cartesian product of the orbits of the
(p_{j})_{j}
, therefore it is also a singleton (resp. bounded). Now, by
lemma

6.2
and quantifier elimination, the map
F\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{j}\pi_{j}(F)
is an equivariant bijection, so the consistent global type corresponding to
(p_{j})_{j}
witnesses the first condition, and we get the bottom-to-top direction.
∎

Note that, given special subgroups
A\leqslant B
, and a tuple
c
,
c
is always
A
-interdefinable with a subtuple
d
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
(choose any of those subtuples, of maximal length), thus there is a natural equivariant homeomorphism between the space of global extensions of
\textup{tp}(c/B)
and that of
\textup{tp}(d/B)
. Corollary
6.7
completes the picture, and gives us easy conditions on
d
to check whether
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
or
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}}B
.

Remark 6.8
.

Corollary
6.7
raises one interesting question: does the orbit of an element of
\prod\limits_{j}\pi_{j}(F)
coincide with the Cartesian product of the orbits of its components ? In other words, for
p_{j}\in\pi_{j}(F)
, is the natural (injective) map:

{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigcap\limits_{j}Stab(p_{j})}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\bigcap\limits_{j}Stab(p_{j})}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{j}Stab(p_{j})}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{j}Stab(p_{j})}$}}}\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{j}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{Stab(p_{j})}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{Stab(p_{j})}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{Stab(p_{j})}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\textup{Aut}({M}/A)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{Stab(p_{j})}$}}}

a bijection ?

7.
Invariance and boundedness of the global extensions of the partial types

Assumptions 7.1
.

Let
{M}\models
ROAG, let
A\leqslant B
be special subgroups of
{M}
, let
\lambda=\max(|B|,2^{\aleph_{0}})^{+}
, let
c=c_{1}\ldots c_{n}
be a tuple from
M
, and suppose that
{M}
is
\lambda
-saturated and strongly
\lambda
-homogeneous. Suppose
c
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
, and let
F
be the closed space of global types whose realizations are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
M
. For each
j\in J
, let
p_{j}=\textup{tp}_{j}(c/B)
. A set of cardinality
\kappa
is called
small
if
{M}
is
\kappa^{+}
-saturated and strongly
\kappa^{+}
-homogeneous, else it is
large
.

By
corollary

6.7
, in order to understand the global extensions of
\textup{tp}(c/B)
which are invariant or have a bounded orbit under the action of
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
, one has to understand, for each
j\in J
the global extensions in
\pi_{j}(F)
of
p_{j}
which are invariant or have a bounded orbit.

7.1.
Partial types using equality and order

Assumptions 7.2
.

In this subsection, on top of
7.1
we fix
\overline{M}\models
DOAG some
|M|^{+}
-saturated, strongly
|M|^{+}
-homogeneous elementary extension of
\textup{div}({M})
.

Remark 7.3
.

Let
d
be some tuple from
M
. Then
d\models p_{<}
if and only if
c
and
d
have the same type over
B
in
\overline{{M}}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
D
be some small special subgroup of
{M}
. Let
\alpha=\alpha_{0}\ldots\alpha_{m-1}
be a tuple from
\overline{{M}}
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
D
. Suppose for all non-zero
f\in\textup{LC}^{m}(\mathbb{Q})
, and for all
d\in D
, we have
f(\alpha)\not\in\left]d,d+\text{1}\right[
(this interval of
\overline{{M}}
being by convention empty when
\text{1}=0
). Then there exists
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}(\overline{{M}}/D)
such that
\sigma(\alpha)
is a tuple from
M
.

Proof.

Suppose by induction we have
\sigma_{i}\in\textup{Aut}(\overline{{M}}/D)
sending
\alpha_{<i}
to a tuple from
M
for some
i<m
(
\sigma_{0}=id
will do for
i=0
). Let
D_{i}
be the special subgroup of
{M}
generated by
D\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{<i})
, which is exactly the relative divisible closure in
M
of
D+\sum\limits_{k<i}\mathbb{Z}\cdot\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{k})
. Then, for all non-zero
f\in\textup{LC}^{n-i}(\mathbb{Q})
, for all
d\in D_{i}
, we have by hypothesis
f(\alpha_{\geqslant i})\not\in\left]d,d+\text{1}\right[
. Let us find
\tau\in\textup{Aut}(\overline{{M}}/D_{i})
such that
\tau(\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{i}))\in M
. Then we could set
\sigma_{i+1}=\tau\circ\sigma_{i}
, and conclude by induction.

By strong homogeneity of
\overline{{M}}
, it is enough to show that any interval of
\overline{{M}}
with bounds in
\textup{div}(D_{i})\cup\{\pm\infty\}
containing
\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{i})
has a point
\beta
in
M
. Let
I
be such an interval. If either the lower or the upper bound of
I
is in
\{\pm\infty\}
, then
\beta
clearly exists, one may choose some multiple of
d
with large enough absolute value if
d\neq 0
, else choose any non-trivial element with correct sign. Now, suppose
I=\left]\dfrac{d_{1}}{N_{1}},\dfrac{d_{2}}{N_{2}}\right[
, with
d_{k}\in D_{i}
,
N_{k}>0
(by the
\mathbb{Q}
-freeness assumption on
\alpha
,
\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{i})\not\in\textup{div}(D_{i})
, thus it does not matter whether the bounds of
I
belong to
I
). Then
I
has a point in
M
if and only if
]N_{2}d_{1},N_{1}d_{2}[
has a point in
N_{1}N_{2}M
. If
{M}
is dense, then
I
has infinitely many points in
M
, and we can use the axioms of ROAG to conclude.

There remains to deal with the case where
{M}
is discrete. We established earlier that the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combinations of
\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{\geqslant i})
do not belong to
]d,d+\text{1}[
for any
d\in D_{i}
. In particular, for every
N\in\mathbb{Z}
:

N_{1}N_{2}\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{i})\not\in\bigcup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{Z}}]N_{2}d_{1}+N\cdot\text{1},N_{2}d_{1}+(N+1)\cdot\text{1}[

and by
\mathbb{Q}
-freeness we also have
N_{1}N_{2}\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{i})\not\in\left\{N_{2}d_{1}+N\cdot\text{1}|N\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}
. As a result,
N_{1}N_{2}\sigma_{i}(\alpha_{i})\in]N_{2}d_{1},N_{1}d_{2}[\setminus]N_{2}d_{1},N_{2}d_{1}+(N_{1}N_{2}+2)\cdot\text{1}]
, which must imply that
N_{2}d_{1}+(N_{1}N_{2}+1)\cdot\text{1}<N_{1}d_{2}
, so
I
has at least
N_{1}N_{2}+1
(in fact, infinitely many) points in
M
, and we can also conclude with the axioms of ROAG.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\alpha\in M^{n}
be a tuple which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
, such that
p=\textup{tp}^{\textup{DOAG}}(\alpha/B)
does not fork over
A
. Let
D
be a small special subgroup of
M
containing
B
, and let
q\in S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(\textup{div}(D))
be an extension of
p
which does not fork over
A
. Then
q
has a realization in
M^{n}
.

Proof.

First of all, the realizations of
q
are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
D
. Let
d\in D
, and let
f
be a non-zero element of
\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q})
. By
lemma

7.4
, one just has to prove that
q(x)\models f(x)\not\in]d,d+\text{1}[
. If not, then by
theorem

1.14
, the interval
[d,d+\text{1}]
must have a point in
\textup{div}(A)
. By multiplying everything by a sufficiently large
N>0
, the interval
]Nd,Nd+N\cdot\text{1}[
has a point in
A
, and
Nd\in D
, thus
Nd\in A
.

In particular, we have
q_{|A}(x)\models Nf(x)\in]Nd,Nd+N\cdot\text{1}[
, therefore
p(x)\models Nf(x)\in]Nd,Nd+N\cdot\text{1}[
, and
Nf(\alpha)\in]Nd,Nd+N\cdot\text{1}[
. As
Nd
and
\alpha
are in
A
, we have
Nf(\alpha)\in A
, contradicting
\mathbb{Q}
-freeness of
\alpha
.
∎

Proposition 7.6
.

By
remark

7.3
, let
h
be the natural injection:

\pi_{<}(F)\longrightarrow S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(\textup{div}(M))

Consider the action of
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
on
Q=\pi_{<}(F)
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

Some element of
Q
is invariant and extends
p_{<}
.

(2)

Some element of
Q
has a bounded orbit and extends
p_{<}
.

(3)

The partial type
p_{<}
does not fork over
A
.

(4)

The partial type
p_{<}
does not divide over
A
.

(5)

Every closed bounded interval with bounds in
B
containing a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of
c
also has a point in
\textup{div}(A)
.

(6)

For some
p\in Q
extending
p_{<}
,
h(p)
extends to some
\textup{Aut}(\overline{{M}}/A)
-invariant type over
\overline{{M}}
.

Proof.

The directions
1\Longrightarrow 2
and
3\Longrightarrow 4
are immediate.

Let us prove
2\Longrightarrow 3
. Let
q_{<}\in Q
witness
2
. For each prime
l
, let
q_{l}(x)
in
S^{n}_{l}(M)
be the partial type
\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}\left(f(x)\right)|n>0,f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z}),f\neq 0\right\}=\textup{tp}_{l}(0/M)
. Note that
q_{l}
is clearly invariant. Then the complete global type in
F
corresponding to
(q_{<},(q_{l})_{l})
(recall
F\longrightarrow\pi_{<}(F)\times\prod\limits_{l\textup{ prime}}S^{n}_{l}(M)
is a homeomorphism by
lemma

6.2
, thus this type is consistent) has a bounded orbit, and thus does not fork over
A
, and we get
3
.

Let us prove
4\Longrightarrow 5
. Suppose we have
b_{k}\in B
,
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})
such that the formula
f(c)\in[b_{1},b_{2}]
witnesses the failure of
5
. Let us show that the formula
f(x)\in[b_{1},b_{2}]
divides over
A
, witnessing the failure of
4
. As in the proof of
corollary

1.8
, it is enough to find
d\equiv_{A}b_{1}
such that
d>b_{2}
. As
[b_{1},b_{2}]
has no point in
\textup{div}(A)
, we have
b_{k}\not\in A
, thus the singletons
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are both
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
. Now, by
lemma

6.2
, one just has to show that
\textup{tp}_{<}(b_{1}/A)
is consistent with
]b_{2},+\infty[
. If not, then all the elements in
M
of
]b_{2},+\infty[
have a type over
A
in
\overline{{M}}
which is distinct from that of
b_{1}
. As a result, by the characterization of
1
-types in DOAG, there must exist in
\overline{{M}}
a point in
\textup{div}(A)\cap]b_{1},b_{2}]
, a contradiction.

Let us show
5\Longrightarrow 6
. Suppose
6
fails. Then, by
theorem

1.14
,
p_{<}
is inconsistent with the partial type
\left\{f(x)\not\in I|I\in\mathcal{I},f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})\right\}
, with
\mathcal{I}
the set of closed bounded intervals with bounds in
M
that have no points in
\textup{div}(A)
. By compactness, there exist finite subsets
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{I}
,
G\subseteq\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
p_{<}(x)\models\bigvee\limits_{I\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime},g\in G}g(x)\in I
. Let
D
be the special subgroup of
{M}
generated by
B
and the bounds of the elements of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. Then, for all
p\in Q
extending
p_{<}
, the restriction of
p
to
D
corresponds to a type in
S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(D)
which forks over
A
in DOAG.

Suppose now, by contradiction, that
5
holds. Let
q
be the type of
S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(B)
corresponding to
p_{<}
. Then
q
does not fork over
A
. By extension, let
q^{\prime}\in S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(D)
be an extension of
q
which does not fork over
A
. By
lemma

7.5
,
q^{\prime}
must admit a realization
\beta
in
M^{n}
(in particular
\beta\models p_{<}
). As
q^{\prime}
does not fork over
A
, we have
g(\beta)\not\in I
for all
g\in G
,
I\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
. As a result,
q_{<}=\textup{tp}_{<}(\beta/D)
cannot be extended to any element of
F
. This means that in any elementary extension of
{M}
, no realization of
q_{<}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
M
. By compactness, this means that there exist finite subsets
P\subseteq M
,
G^{\prime}\subseteq\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q})\setminus\{0\}
, such that
q_{<}(x)\models\bigvee\limits_{m\in P,g\in G^{\prime}}g(x)=m
.

Then we can reach a contradiction by extending
q^{\prime}
once more: let
\tilde{D}
be the special subgroup of
{M}
generated by
D\cup P
, let
\tilde{q}\in S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(\tilde{D})
be an extension of
q^{\prime}
which does not fork over
A
. Then with the same argument,
\tilde{q}
has a realization
\gamma\in M^{n}
, and by hypothesis
\gamma
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
\tilde{D}
, a contradiction with the fact that
\tilde{q}
does not fork over
A
.

Suppose
p\in Q
witnesses
6
, and let us show that
p
is
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
-invariant, which would allow us to conclude the whole proof with the direction
6\Longrightarrow 1
. Let
q\in S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(\overline{{M}})
be some global
\textup{Aut}(\overline{{M}}/A)
-invariant extension of
h(p)
, and
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
. Then
\sigma
extends uniquely to
\sigma^{\prime}
, an automorphism of the ordered group
\textup{div}(M)
, which fixes
A
pointwise. We clearly have
h(\sigma(p))=\sigma^{\prime}(h(p))
(look at the atomic formulas with predicate
=,<
that belong to
X_{<}
, check that their image by
\sigma
is satisfied by the realizations of
\sigma^{\prime}(h(p))
). By quantifier elimination in DOAG,
\sigma^{\prime}
is a partial elementary map in
\overline{M}
. By strong homogeneity of
\overline{{M}}
,
\sigma^{\prime}
extends to some
\tilde{\sigma}\in\textup{Aut}(\overline{{M}})
. As
\sigma^{\prime}
pointwise-fixes
A
, so does
\tilde{\sigma}
, thus
\tilde{\sigma}(q)=q
. Now, we conclude that:
\sigma(p)=h^{-1}(\sigma^{\prime}(h(p))=h^{-1}(\sigma^{\prime}(q_{|\textup{div}(M)}))=h^{-1}(\tilde{\sigma}(q_{|\textup{div}(M)}))=h^{-1}(\tilde{\sigma}(q)_{|\tilde{\sigma}(\textup{div}(M))})=h^{-1}(q_{|\textup{div}(M)})=h^{-1}(h(p))=p
.
∎

The fifth condition of
proposition

7.6
is a very simple geometric condition, the kind of statement that would be very satisfactory for a characterization of forking. In the subsections
7.3
and
7.4
, we look for similar conditions for the
(p_{l})_{l}
.

7.2.
Mapping monsters to monsters

Remark 7.7
.

Up to isomorphism, the unique discrete subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
is
\mathbb{Z}
, and all its elements are
\emptyset
-definable in
\mathcal{L}_{P}
. As a result, any discrete model of ROAG is an elementary extension of
\mathbb{Z}
, which we identify with
\textup{dcl}(\emptyset)
.

If
d
is a tuple in some elementary extension of
M
, then
proposition

7.6
shows that
p_{<}
does not fork/divide over
A
if and only if the corresponding partial type (via
h
) in
S_{\textup{DOAG}}(\textup{div}(M))
does not fork over
A
. Moreover, one can easily see that
h
is continuous, and the restriction of
h
to the closed subspace of
\pi_{<}(F)
of extensions of
p_{<}
which do not fork over
A
is, by
lemma

7.5
, onto the closed subspace of
S(\textup{div}(M))
of extensions of
h(p_{<})
which do not fork over
A
. It follows that they are homeomorphic.

One may think that we just proved that this topological space is homeomorphic to the one described in
remark

3.31
, but there is actually a subtle obstruction, coming from the fact that
\textup{div}(M)
is not necessarily
|B|^{+}
-saturated. For the remainder of this subsection, we show that the space of extensions of
p_{<}
which do not fork over
A
is indeed homeomorphic to the space described
remark

3.31
. For this, we have to do a case disjunction depending on whether
M
is dense or not.

If
M
is dense, we show that there exists a sufficiently saturated elementary extension
N
of
M
such that
\textup{div}(N)
is also sufficiently saturated.

If
M
is discrete, then
\textup{div}(M)
is never
\aleph_{1}
-saturated, for it does not admit non-zero elements having smaller Archimedean value than
1
. The homeomorphism
h
that we build does not come from the divisible closure, but from the quotient
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}}
, which is a model of DOAG. We show that this structure is sufficiently saturated.

Note that we do not care about strong homogeneity here. Indeed, one may easily see that a partial global
A
-invariant type on an
|A|^{+}
-saturated first-order structure extends uniquely to an
A
-invariant type over any
|A|^{+}
-saturated elementary extension, regardless of strong homogeneity.

Definition 7.8
.

Suppose
M
is discrete. We define
\widetilde{M}
as the quotient
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}}$}}}
, and
\pi
the canonical surjection. As
\mathbb{Z}
is a convex subgroup,
\widetilde{M}
is naturally an ordered Abelian group by
definition

1.2
, and
\pi
is an order-preserving map.

Proposition 7.9
.

If
M
is discrete, then
\widetilde{M}\models\textup{DOAG}
.

Proof.

By saturation,
M\neq\mathbb{Z}
, therefore
\widetilde{M}
is non-trivial. Let
\pi(a)\in\widetilde{M}
, and
m>0
. The quotient and remainder for the Euclidean division by
m
are
\emptyset
-definable functions in
M
, therefore there exists
b\in M
such that
a=m\cdot b+k\cdot\text{1}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
(
k<m
), in particular
\pi(a)=m\cdot\pi(b)
, concluding the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.10
.

The map
h^{\prime}:\ \textup{tp}_{<}(c/M)\longrightarrow\textup{tp}\left(\pi(c)/\widetilde{M}\right)
is a well-defined continuous injection
\pi_{<}(F)\longrightarrow S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}\left(\widetilde{M}\right)
.

Proof.

Let
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q})
,
b\in M
, and
c
,
d\in M^{n}
such that
c
and
d
are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
\textup{dcl}(b)
. Let
m>0
be such that
m\cdot f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Let us show that
h^{\prime}
is well-defined. If
\textup{tp}_{<}(c/\textup{dcl}(b))=\textup{tp}_{<}(d/\textup{dcl}(b))
, then:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}f(\pi(c))>\pi(b)&\Longleftrightarrow&m\cdot f(\pi(c))>m\cdot\pi(b)\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\ m\cdot f(c)+k\cdot\text{1}>b\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\ m\cdot f(d)+k\cdot\text{1}>b\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&f(\pi(d))>\pi(b)\end{array}

\begin{array}[]{rcl}f(\pi(c))=\pi(b)&\Longleftrightarrow&m\cdot f(\pi(c))=m\cdot\pi(b)\\
&\Longleftrightarrow&m\cdot f(c)\in(m\cdot b+\mathbb{Z})\\
&\Longrightarrow&m\cdot f(c)\in\textup{dcl}(b)\\
&\Longrightarrow&\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f=0\\
b\in\mathbb{Z}\end{array}\right.\textup{ by }\mathbb{Q}\textup{-freeness}\\
&\Longrightarrow&f(\pi(d))=\pi(b)\end{array}

it follows that
\textup{tp}(\pi(c)/\pi(\textup{dcl}(b)))=\textup{tp}(\pi(d)/\pi(\textup{dcl}(b)))
, therefore
h^{\prime}
is well-defined. The above computations also show us that
h^{\prime}
is continuous.

Now, if
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, then, by
\mathbb{Q}
-freeness, we have:

f(c)>b\Longleftrightarrow\forall k\in\mathbb{Z}\ f(c)>b+k\cdot\text{1}\Longleftrightarrow f(\pi(c))>\pi(b)

and:

f(c)=b\Longrightarrow f=0\Longrightarrow f(\pi(c))=0

it follows that
h^{\prime}
is an injection.
∎

Lemma 7.11
.

Suppose that
M
is discrete. Let
h^{\prime}
be the map from
lemma

7.10
, and let
q\in\pi_{<}(F)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

For all
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, for all closed bounded intervals
I
with bounds in
M
having no point in
\textup{div}(A)
,
q(x)\models f(x)\not\in I
.

(2)

For all
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Q})
, for all closed bounded intervals
I
with bounds in
\pi(M)
having no point in
\pi(A)
,
h^{\prime}(q)(x)\models f(x)\not\in I
.

Proof.

Suppose
1
fails, and let
f
,
I
be a witness. As
A
is a special subgroup, we have
\mathbb{Z}\subseteq A
, therefore the interval:

[\min(I)-m\cdot\text{1},\max(I)+m\cdot\text{1}]

has no point in
A
for every
m<\omega
. It follows that:

I^{\prime}=[\pi(\min(I)),\pi(\max(I))]

has no point in
\pi(A)
. As a result,
f
,
I^{\prime}
is a witness of the failure of
2
.

Conversely, suppose
2
fails, and let
f,I
be a witness. We must have
f\neq 0
, otherwise
I
would have a point in
\pi(A)
. Let
b_{1}
,
b_{2}
be respective preimages of
\min(I)
,
\max(I)
by
\pi
. Let
m>0
be such that
m\cdot f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
. As
q\in\pi_{<}(F)
, and
f\neq 0
,
q(x)\models m\cdot f(x)\not\in M
. This would not be the case if we had
m\cdot\pi(b_{1})=m\cdot\pi(b_{2})
, therefore we have
m\cdot\pi(b_{1})<m\cdot\pi(b_{2})
, thus
m\cdot b_{1}<m\cdot b_{2}
, and
q(x)\models m\cdot f(x)\in I^{\prime}=[m\cdot b_{1},m\cdot b_{2}]
. In order to conclude the proof, it suffices to show that
I^{\prime}
has no point in
\textup{div}(A)
, which would establish the failure of
1
, with the witness
m\cdot f
,
I^{\prime}
. Let
a\in A
, and
k>0
. If we had
\dfrac{a}{k}\in I^{\prime}
, then we would have
\dfrac{1}{mk}\pi(a)\in I
. However, as
A
is definably closed, and the quotient for the Euclidean division by
mk
is
\emptyset
-definable, we would have
\dfrac{1}{mk}\pi(a)\in\pi(A)
, a contradiction.
∎

Corollary 7.12
.

Suppose
M
is discrete, and let
q\in\pi_{<}(F)
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

q
does not fork over
A
.

(2)

h^{\prime}(q)
does not fork over
\pi(A)
.

Proof.

By condition
5
of
proposition

7.6
,
q
does not fork over
A
if and only if condition
1
of
lemma

7.11
holds. By
definition

1.12
, the second condition of
lemma

7.11
exactly states that
h^{\prime}(q)
avoids all the definable sets witnessing cut-dependence (i.e. forking-dependence by
theorem

1.14
) from
\pi(M)
over
\pi(A)
, therefore
h^{\prime}(q)
does not fork over
\pi(A)
if and only if condition
2
of
lemma

7.11
holds.
∎

Lemma 7.13
.

Suppose
M
is discrete. Let
q\in S^{n}_{\textup{DOAG}}(\widetilde{M})
be the type of a family which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
\widetilde{M}
. Then there exists
p\in\pi_{<}(F)
such that
h^{\prime}(p)=q
.

Proof.

Let
I
be a finite set. For each
i\in I
, let
b_{i},b^{\prime}_{i}\in M
, and let
f_{i}\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Q})
, such that
q(x)\models\pi(b_{i})<f_{i}(x)<\pi(b^{\prime}_{i})
. By compactness, it suffices to find a tuple
d
in
M
such that
\pi(b_{i})<f_{i}(\pi(d))<\pi(b^{\prime}_{i})
for every
i
. By consistency of
q
, there exists a tuple
\delta
in
\widetilde{M}
such that
\pi(b_{i})<f_{i}(\delta)<\pi(b^{\prime}_{i})
for every
i
. We simply choose
d
an arbitrary lift of
\delta
by
\pi
.
∎

It follows that every type over
\widetilde{M}
which extends
h^{\prime}(p_{<})
and which does not fork over
\pi(A)
has a lift in
\pi_{<}(F)
by
h^{\prime}
which does not fork over
A
. As a result,
h^{\prime}
restricts to a homeomorphism between the space of types in
\pi_{<}(F)
which extend
p_{<}
and which do not fork over
A
, and the space of types in
S_{\textup{DOAG}}(\widetilde{M})
which extend
h^{\prime}(p_{<})
and which do not fork over
\pi(A)
.

Now we show that
\textup{div}(M)
or
\widetilde{M}
is sufficiently saturated:

Proposition 7.14
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal such that
M
is
\kappa
-saturated. If
M
is dense, then
\textup{div}(M)
is
\kappa
-saturated.

Proof.

Suppose
M
is dense. Let
(I_{i})_{i}
be a consistent family of strictly less than
\kappa
many intervals from
\textup{div}(M)
. It suffices to show that
\bigcap\limits_{i}I_{i}
has a point in
\textup{div}(M)
(as saturation for unary types implies saturation). We may assume every intersection of finitely many intervals from the family is also in the family. If some
I_{i}
has empty interior, then it is a singleton, and the proof is trivial, therefore we may assume that every
I_{i}
is open of non-empty interior.

As
M
is
\kappa
-saturated and dense, there exists a non-zero
\varepsilon\in M
such that
\Delta(\varepsilon)<\Delta(\sup(I_{i})-\inf(I_{i}))
(
\Delta
is defined in
definition

1.24
) for all
i
. In particular:

\inf(I_{i})<\inf(I_{i})+|\varepsilon|<\sup(I_{i})-|\varepsilon|<\sup(I_{i})

For each
i
, let
m_{i}<\omega
be sufficiently large, such that
m_{i}\cdot\inf(I_{i})
,
m_{i}\cdot(\inf(I_{i})+|\varepsilon|)
,
m_{i}\cdot(\sup(I_{i})-|\varepsilon|)
,
m_{i}\cdot\sup(I_{i})
are all in
M
. By regularity, as both the intervals
]m_{i}\cdot\inf(I_{i}),m_{i}\cdot(\inf(I_{i})+|\varepsilon|)[
and
]m_{i}\cdot(\sup(I_{i})-|\varepsilon|),m_{i}\cdot\sup(I_{i})[
are infinite, they have a point in
m_{i}\cdot M
, therefore there exists
a_{i},b_{i}\in M
such that:

\inf(I_{i})<a_{i}<\inf(I_{i})+|\varepsilon|<\sup(I_{i})-|\varepsilon|<b_{i}<\sup(I_{i})

Let
I^{\prime}_{i}=]a_{i},b_{i}[
, which is an interval of
M
. We have
I^{\prime}_{i}\subseteq I_{i}
for every
i
. It suffices to show that the family
(I^{\prime}_{i})_{i}
has a point in
M
. By saturation and density, it suffices to show that
a_{i}<b_{j}
for every
i,j
. It suffices to show that
\inf(I_{i})+|\varepsilon|<\sup(I_{j})-|\varepsilon|
. As
(I_{i})_{i}
is closed under finite intersection, and by definition of
\varepsilon
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}2\cdot|\varepsilon|&<&\sup(I_{i}\cap I_{j})-\inf(I_{i}\cap I_{j})\\
&=&\min(\sup(I_{i}),\sup(I_{j}))-\max(\inf(I_{i}),\inf(I_{j}))\\
&\leqslant&\sup(I_{j})-\inf(I_{i})\end{array}

hence the result is proved.
∎

Proposition 7.15
.

Let
\kappa
be an infinite cardinal such that
M
is
\kappa
-saturated, and suppose
M
is discrete. Then
\widetilde{M}
is
\kappa
-saturated.

Proof.

Let
(I_{i})_{i}
be a consistent family of strictly less than
\kappa
many open intervals of non-empty interior of
\widetilde{M}
. Let
a_{i},b_{i}\in M
be such that
I_{i}=]\pi(a_{i}),\pi(b_{i})[
. Then the family of every interval of the form
]a_{i}+k\cdot\text{1},b_{i}+k^{\prime}\cdot\text{1}[
, with
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
is consistent in
M
. It can be defined with as many parameters as the initial family, therefore its intersection has a point
d\in M
. We clearly have
\pi(d)\in\bigcap\limits_{i}I_{i}
, concluding the proof.
∎

Proposition 7.16
.

The space of types in
\pi_{<}(F)
which extend
p_{<}
and do not fork over
A
is homeomorphic to the space described in
remark

3.31
.

Proof.

The homeomorphism is given by
h
if
M
is dense, and
h^{\prime}
if it is discrete.
∎

7.3.
Partial types using a prime of finite index

For the rest of this section, we fix a prime
l
.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
G
be a torsion-free Abelian group. Then, for all
N>0
, we have in
G^{eq}
a
\emptyset
-definable group isomorphism between the two definable groups
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\displaystyle{lG}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{lG}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43054pt}{$\scriptstyle{lG}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7361pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{lG}$}}}
and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{l^{N}G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{l^{N+1}G}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{l^{N}G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{l^{N+1}G}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{l^{N}G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{l^{N+1}G}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{l^{N}G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{l^{N+1}G}$}}}
.

Proof.

Let
x,y\in G
, and
N>0
. Suppose
l^{N}x-l^{N}y\in l^{N+1}G
. Then, as
G
is torsion-free, we have
x-y\in lG
, thus the map:

f:\ l^{N}x\textup{ mod }l^{N+1}G\longmapsto x\textup{ mod }lG

is a well-defined group homomorphism, which is clearly surjective. Now, if
x\not\in lG
, then
l^{N}x\not\in l^{N+1}G
, so the map is injective.
∎

Corollary 7.18
.

If
lG
has a finite index
d
, then
\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G
has index at most equal to
2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Proof.

Define a tree structure on
\bigcup\limits_{N\geqslant 0}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{l^{N}G}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{l^{N}G}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{l^{N}G}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{l^{N}G}$}}}
of root
G
, such that the parent of some node
x\textup{ mod }l^{N+1}G
is
x\textup{ mod }l^{N}G
. Then every node has
d
children and the tree has
\aleph_{0}
levels, thus it admits at most
2^{\aleph_{0}}
branches. To conclude, the map
x\textup{ mod }\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G\longmapsto(x\textup{ mod }l^{N}G)_{N}
is a natural bijection between
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{G}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}G}$}}}
and the set of the branches.
∎

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
D
be some small special subgroup of
{M}
. Suppose we have
e_{1},e_{2}\in M
, such that
e_{1},e_{2}\not\in D+l^{N}M
, and
e_{1}-e_{2}\in l^{N-1}M
, for some
N>0
. Then we have
\textup{tp}_{l}(e_{1}/D)=\textup{tp}_{l}(e_{2}/D)
.

Proof.

Any atomic formula with parameters in
D
and predicate in
\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{m}}|m>0\right\}
can clearly be written
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{m}}(\lambda x-d)
, with
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z},m>0,d\in D
. We have several cases:

•

\lambda\in l\mathbb{Z}
,
d\not\in lM
, in which case the formula always fails.

•

\lambda\in l\mathbb{Z}
,
d\in lM
,
m=1
, in which case the formula always holds.

•

\lambda\not\in l\mathbb{Z}
, in which case
\lambda
and
l
are coprime. Therefore, by Bézout’s identities, the formula is equivalent to
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{m}}(x-d^{\prime})
for some
d^{\prime}\in D
. By hypothesis on the
e_{i}
, this formula is satisfied by
e_{1}
if and only if it is satisfied by
e_{2}
.

•

\lambda\in l\mathbb{Z}
,
d\in lM
,
m>1
, in which case the formula is equivalent to
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{m-1}}\left(\dfrac{\lambda}{l}x-\dfrac{d}{l}\right)
, and we reduce by induction to one of the above three cases.

either way, we clearly see that those formulas are satisfied by
e_{1}
if and only if they are satisfied by
e_{2}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.20
.

Let
d
be a tuple from
M
, which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
. Then
d\models p_{l}
if and only if, for each
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, for each
N>0
, either we have
f(c)-f(d)\in l^{N}M
, or neither
f(c)
nor
f(d)
are in
B+l^{N}M
.

Proof.

Any atomic formula
\varphi(x_{1}\ldots x_{n})
with predicate in
\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}|N>0\right\}
can be written
\psi\left(f(x)\right)
, with
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})
, and
\psi
an atomic formula on the same predicate with one variable. As a result,
d\models p_{l}
if and only if
\textup{tp}_{l}\left(f(d)/B\right)=\textup{tp}_{l}\left(f(c)/B\right)
for all
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})
. Fix
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Suppose that we have
f(c)\in B+l^{N}M
for all
N>0
. For each
N
, let
b_{N}\in B
such that
f(c)-b_{N}\in l^{N}M
. Then
\textup{tp}_{l}(f(c)/B)
contains
\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(x-b_{N})|N>0\right\}
. This inclusion is an equality, for if
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(f(d)-b_{N})
for all
N
, then we have
f(d)-f(c)\in l^{N}M
for all
N
, which clearly implies
\textup{tp}_{l}(f(c)/B)=\textup{tp}_{l}(f(d)/B)
. In particular,
\textup{tp}_{l}(f(d)/B)=\textup{tp}_{l}(f(c)/B)
if and only if
f(d)-f(c)\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
.

Suppose now that there exists
N>0
such that
f(c)\not\in B+l^{N}M
. The set of all such
N
is a final segment of
\omega
, choose
N
its least element. Let
b\in B
such that
f(c)-b\in l^{N-1}M
(if
N=1
, then
b=0
will do). Then, by
lemma

7.19
,
\textup{tp}_{l}(f(c)/B)
is generated by the partial type:

\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N-1}}(x-b)\}\cup\left\{\neg\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(x-b^{\prime})|b^{\prime}\in B\right\}

this concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

If
[M:lM]
is finite, then by
corollary

7.18
the
\emptyset
-type-definable equivalence relation
\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(x-y)|N>0\right\}
is bounded (has a small number of classes) and finer than the equivalence relation of having the same
l
-type over any parameter set, thus
S^{n}_{l}(M)
is small, and each orbit of
S^{n}_{l}(M)
under
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
is obviously bounded.

Moreover, the classes of this
\emptyset
-type-definable equivalence relation are all
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant, thus
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
acts trivially over
S^{n}_{l}(M)
.

Let us recall that we adopted
7.1
in the section.

Proposition 7.22
.

Suppose
[M:lM]
is finite. Consider the action of
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
on
Q=S^{n}_{l}(M)
. Then
p_{l}
has an invariant extension in
Q
if and only if every
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of
c
belongs to
\bigcap\limits_{N}(A+l^{N}M)
.

Proof.

Suppose every
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combination of
c
belongs to
\bigcap\limits_{N}(A+l^{N}M)
. Then
p_{l}(M)
can be written as the intersection of
A
-definable sets of the form:

\left\{x|\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}\left(f(x)-a\right)\right\},

with
N>0,a\in A
,
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})
. As a result,
p_{l}(M)
is
A
-type definable (thus
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant), and all its realizations
d\in M^{n}
satisfy the conditions
f(c)-f(d)\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
for all
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
. By
corollary

7.20
,
p_{l}
is complete as an element of
Q
, so we get the right-to-left direction.

Suppose now that we have
f\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
and
k>0
such that
f(c)
does not belong to
A+l^{k}M
. Let
p\in Q
be an extension of
p_{l}
. By compactness and finiteness of
[M:lM]
, let
\alpha\in M
be such that
p(x)\models\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}\left(f(x)-\alpha\right)
for all
N>0
. As
[M:A+l^{k}M]>1
, we must have by
proposition

7.17
the inequalities
1<[M:l^{k}M]=[M:lM]^{k}
, thus
1<[M:lM]=[l^{k}M:l^{k+1}M]
, so let
m\in l^{k}M\setminus l^{k+1}M
. Then, by
lemma

7.19
,
\textup{tp}_{l}(\alpha/A)=\textup{tp}_{l}(\alpha+m/A)
. Let
D
be a special subgroup of
{M}
of size
\leqslant\max(2^{\aleph_{0}},|A|)
containing
A
, and a system of representatives of the cosets of
\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
. By saturation of
M
, we can find
\beta\in M
such that
\textup{tp}_{l}(\beta/D)=\textup{tp}_{l}(\alpha-m/D)
(which implies
\beta-\alpha-m\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
), and
\textup{tp}_{j}(\beta/D)=\textup{tp}_{j}(\alpha/D)
for all
j\neq l
(which implies
\textup{tp}_{j}(\beta/A)=\textup{tp}_{j}(\alpha/A)
). Then, by strong homogeneity of
M
, there exists
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
such that
\sigma(\alpha)=\beta
. Now, we have
\sigma(p)(x)\models\mathfrak{d}_{l^{k+1}}\left(f(x)-\alpha-m\right)
, a formula that is inconsistent with
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{k+1}}\left(f(x)-\alpha\right)
. As a result,
\sigma(p)\neq p
, concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 7.23
.

Just like in subsection
7.2
, we would like to describe the space of global invariant/bounded extensions of
p_{l}
. In case the condition of
proposition

7.22
holds, we saw that
p_{l}
is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant and complete in
S^{n}_{l}(M)
, thus this space is a single point. In case the condition does not hold, the space of invariant extensions of
p_{l}
is obviously empty, but we would like to describe the topological space of all the extensions that have a bounded orbit. This is what we do in the remainder of this subsection, we exhibit a homeomorphism between this orbit and some closed subspace of the Cantor space.

Definition 7.24
.

Let
N>0
. We define
\mathcal{T}_{N}
the tree
\{0,\ldots N-1\}^{<\omega}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{N}=\{0,\ldots N-1\}^{\omega}
the set of its branches. The
m
-th-level of
\mathcal{T}_{N}
is the set
\{0,\ldots N-1\}^{m}
. We see
\mathcal{B}_{N}
as a topological space, with the elementary open sets being the
O_{w}=\{b\in\mathcal{B}_{N}|w\textup{ is a prefix of }b\}
for every
w\in\mathcal{T}_{N}
. For every
m
, the opens
(O_{w})_{w\in\{0,\ldots N-1\}^{m}}
form a finite disjoint open cover of
\mathcal{B}_{N}
, thus each
O_{w}
is in fact clopen.

Lemma 7.25
.

For all
N,d>0
,
\mathcal{B}_{N}
is naturally homeomorphic to
\mathcal{B}_{N^{d}}
.

Proof.

The natural bijection
\{0,\ldots N^{d}-1\}\longrightarrow\{0,\ldots N-1\}^{d}
extends to a bijection
h:\ \mathcal{B}_{N^{d}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{N}
. For each
m<\omega
, the sets:

\left\{h(O_{w})|w\textup{ in the }m\textup{-th level of }\mathcal{T}_{N^{d}}\right\}

\left\{O_{w}|w\textup{ in the }md\textup{-th level of }\mathcal{T}_{N}\right\},

coincide, thus
h^{-1}
is continuous. To conclude, for each
w\in\mathcal{T}_{N}
,
O_{w}
can be written as a finite union of elementary opens
O_{v}
, with
v
in the
d\lceil|w|/d\rceil
-th level of
\mathcal{T}_{N}
, thus
h^{-1}(O_{w})
can be written as a finite union of some
O_{v}
, with
v
in the
\lceil|w|/d\rceil
-th level of
\mathcal{T}_{N^{d}}
, therefore
h
is continuous.
∎

Lemma 7.26
.

The two spaces
\mathcal{B}_{l}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{l}
(the topological space of
l
-adic integers) are naturally homeomorphic.

Recall that with our conventions, the
l
-adic valuation of
l^{m}
is
-m
.

Proof.

The tree of non-empty balls of
\mathbb{Z}_{l}
(including the whole
\mathbb{Z}_{l}
) ordered by inclusion is isomorphic to
\mathcal{T}_{l}
via the isomorphism mapping each
w\in\mathcal{T}_{l}
to the ball of radius
-|w|
around
\sum\limits_{m}w_{m}l^{m}
.
The corresponding bijection
\mathcal{B}_{l}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{l}
is clearly
h:\ w\longmapsto\sum\limits_{m<\omega}w_{m}l^{m}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.27
.

Suppose
[M:lM]=l^{d}
is finite. Then
S^{n}_{l}(M)
is naturally homeomorphic to the direct product
\mathcal{B}_{l^{d}}^{n}
(with
n=|c|
).

Proof.

Note that each point of
S^{n}_{l}(M)
is a partial type that can be written
\left\{x\in Y_{m}|m<\omega\right\}
, with
Y_{m}\in\left({\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.5714pt}{$\displaystyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58528pt}{$\scriptstyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7986pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{l^{m}M}$}}}\right)^{n}
. In particular,
S^{n}_{l}(M)
is in natural bijection with
\left({\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.5714pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58528pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7986pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}}\right)^{n}
. For any
\mathcal{L}_{P}(M)
-term
t(x)
, the definable subset of
M
defined by
\mathfrak{d}_{l^{m}}(t(x))
is a union of cosets
\textup{ mod }l^{m}M
, thus the elementary clopen subsets of
\left({\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.5714pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58528pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7986pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m<\omega}l^{m}M}$}}}\right)^{n}
induced by
S^{n}_{l}(M)
are the Cartesian products of cosets of
l^{m}M
for
m<\omega
. We build by induction a sequence of bijections
(f_{m})_{m}
between the
m
-th level of
\mathcal{T}_{l^{d}}
and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.5714pt}{$\displaystyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58528pt}{$\scriptstyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7986pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{l^{m}M}$}}}
. Suppose by induction hypothesis that we have
f_{m}
(
f_{0}
is the bijection between the point
\left\{{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}}}\right\}
and the point
\{\emptyset\}
). For each
w
in the
m
-th level,
w
has exactly
l^{d}
children in the
m+1
-th level, and
f_{m}(w)
is the union of exactly
l^{d}
cosets
\textup{ mod }l^{m+1}M
, so there is a bijection between them for each
w
, and we set
f_{m+1}
as the union of those bijections. Then the
(f_{m})_{m}
define an homeomorphism between
\mathcal{B}_{l^{d}}
and
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.5714pt}{$\displaystyle{\bigcap\limits_{m}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58528pt}{$\scriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m}l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7986pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\bigcap\limits_{m}l^{m}M}$}}}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 7.28
.

The closed space of every global extension of
p_{l}
of bounded orbit under the action of
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
is naturally homeomorphic to some closed subspace of
\mathbb{Z}_{l}^{n}
.

7.4.
Partial types using a prime of infinite index

We still fix a prime
l
, and we assume here that
[M:lM]
is infinite.

Lemma 7.29
.

Let
D
be a small special subgroup of
{M}
, and
\alpha=\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{m}
a finite tuple from
M
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
D
. Let
\beta=\beta_{1}\ldots\beta_{m}
be another tuple from
M
such that
\textup{tp}_{l}(\beta/D)=\textup{tp}_{l}(\alpha/D)
. Then there exists
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\ldots\gamma_{m}\equiv_{D}\alpha
such that
\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
for all
i
.

Proof.

As
\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
is large, choose by induction
\varepsilon_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
such that
\varepsilon_{i}
is not in the special subgroup generated by
D\alpha\beta\varepsilon_{<i}
, and let
\beta_{i}^{\prime}=\beta_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}
. Then we have
\beta_{i}-\beta_{i}^{\prime}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
, and
\varepsilon
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over the special subgroup generated by
D\alpha\beta
. As a result,
\alpha
(and hence
(\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}^{\prime})_{i}
) is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
D^{\prime}
, the special subgroup generated by
D\beta^{\prime}
. By
lemma

6.2
, there exists in
M
a tuple
e=e_{1}\ldots e_{m}
such that
e_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
for all
i
, and
\textup{tp}_{j}(e/D^{\prime})=\textup{tp}_{j}((\alpha_{i}-\beta^{\prime}_{i})_{i}/D^{\prime})
for all
j\neq l
. Let
\gamma_{i}=\beta_{i}^{\prime}+e_{i}
. Then, for all
j\neq l
, we have
\textup{tp}_{j}(\gamma/D^{\prime})=\textup{tp}_{j}(\alpha/D^{\prime})
. Moreover, we have
\gamma_{i}-\beta_{i}^{\prime}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
for all
i
, thus
\textup{tp}_{l}(\gamma/D)=\textup{tp}_{l}(\beta^{\prime}/D)=\textup{tp}_{l}(\beta/D)=\textup{tp}_{l}(\alpha/D)
, so we conclude that
\gamma\equiv_{D}\alpha
.
∎

Remark 7.30
.

Recall that any discrete model of ROAG is an elementary extension of
\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, if
[M:lM]
is infinite for some
l
, then
M
must be dense. In that case, the special subgroups of
{M}
(which are exactly the definably closed sets) are its pure subgroups.

Lemma 7.31
.

Let
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
be a finite tuple from
M
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
. Suppose that for each
i\in I
, there exists
N>0
such that
\alpha_{i}\not\in B+l^{N}M
, and choose
N_{i}
the least of those integers for each
i\in I
. Then, there exists
(\beta_{i})_{i}\equiv_{B}(\alpha_{i})_{i}
such that
\beta_{i}-\alpha_{k}\not\in l^{N_{i}}M
for each
i,k\in I
.

Proof.

Note that by
remark

7.30
, the special subgroups of
{M}
are exactly its pure subgroups.

Let
b_{i}\in B
such that
b_{i}-\alpha_{i}\in l^{N_{i}-1}M
. Suppose we can find a witness
(\gamma_{i})_{i}
of the statement with
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
replaced by
\left(\dfrac{\alpha_{i}-b_{i}}{l^{N_{i}-1}}\right)_{i}
(
N_{i}
will be replaced by
1
). Then we can choose
\beta_{i}=l^{N_{i}-1}\gamma_{i}+b_{i}
. For the remainder of the proof, we can suppose without loss that
N_{i}=1
for every
i
(in particular,
b_{i}=0
).

Let
V
be the large
\mathbb{F}_{l}
-vector space
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\displaystyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43054pt}{$\scriptstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7361pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{lM}$}}}
, and
U\leqslant V
the
\mathbb{F}_{l}
-vector subspace
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{(B+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\displaystyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{(B+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{(B+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43054pt}{$\scriptstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{(B+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7361pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{lM}$}}}
. Note that
U
is small, as it is the image by
B
of the map
M\longrightarrow{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\displaystyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43054pt}{$\scriptstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7361pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{lM}$}}}
. Note also that we have
\alpha_{i}\in V\setminus U
for every
i\in I
. Now, let
I_{0}\subseteq I
such that
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}
is a lift of an
\mathbb{F}_{l}
-basis of the image in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{U}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{U}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{U}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{U}$}}}
of
U+\sum\limits_{i\in I}\mathbb{F}_{l}\cdot(\alpha_{i}\textup{ mod }lM)
. Let
D
be the special subgroup of
{M}
generated by
B(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
, and let
U^{\prime}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{(D+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\displaystyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{(D+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{(D+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.43054pt}{$\scriptstyle{lM}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{(D+lM)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7361pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{lM}$}}}
, a small vector subspace of
V
containing
U
. As
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.00891pt}{$\displaystyle{U^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75891pt}{$\textstyle{U^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.8978pt}{$\scriptstyle{U^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.11111pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{U^{\prime}}$}}}
is large, one can choose an arbitrary family
(\beta_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}\in M^{I_{0}}
whose image in
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.00891pt}{$\displaystyle{U^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75891pt}{$\textstyle{U^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.8978pt}{$\scriptstyle{U^{\prime}}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{V}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.11111pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{U^{\prime}}$}}}
is
\mathbb{F}_{l}
-free. Now, for each non-zero
f\in\textup{LC}^{|I_{0}|}(\mathbb{Z})
, if
m
is the largest integer such that all the coefficients of
f
lie in
l^{m}\mathbb{Z}
, then we have:

f((\beta_{i})_{i\in I_{0}})\in l^{m}M\ni f((\alpha_{i})_{i\in I_{0}})

f((\beta_{i})_{i\in I_{0}})\not\in B+l^{m+1}M\not\ni f((\alpha_{i})_{i\in I_{0}})

thus we have
\textup{tp}_{l}((\alpha_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}/B)=\textup{tp}_{l}((\beta_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}/B)
by the same reasoning as in
corollary

7.20
. For each
i\in I_{0}
, as
\beta_{i}\textup{ mod }lM\not\in U^{\prime}
, we have
\beta_{i}\not\in D+lM
, thus
\beta_{i}-\alpha_{k}\not\in lM
for each
k\in I
. By
lemma

7.29
, we can suppose without loss that
(\beta_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}\equiv_{B}(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I_{0}}
.

Choose
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}({M}/B)
such that
\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\beta_{i}
for every
i\in I_{0}
. Let
\beta_{i}=\sigma(\alpha_{i})
for each
i\in I\setminus I_{0}
. Then we have
(\beta_{i})_{i\in I}\equiv_{B}(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
. In order to conclude, we need to show that
\beta_{i}-\alpha_{k}\not\in lM
for each
i\in I\setminus I_{0}
,
k\in I
. Choose
i\in I\setminus I_{0}
. Then there must exist
f_{i}\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\alpha_{i}-f_{i}((\alpha_{k})_{k\in I_{0}})\in B+lM
. Let
e_{i}\in B
be such that
\alpha_{i}-f_{i}((\alpha_{k})_{k\in I_{0}})-e_{i}
lies in
lM
. Then
\beta_{i}-f_{i}((\beta_{k})_{k\in I_{0}})-e_{i}\in lM
, so we just have to show that
f_{i}((\beta_{k})_{k\in I_{0}})\not\in D+lM
. As
\alpha_{i}\not\in B+lM
, some coefficient of
f_{i}
must be coprime with
l
, which concludes the proof as
(\beta_{k}\textup{ mod }lM)_{k\in I_{0}}
is
\mathbb{F}_{l}
-free over
U^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 7.32
.

Let
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
be some tuple from
M
. Then there exists in
M
a tuple
(\beta_{i})_{i}
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
, and for which
\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
for every
i
.

Proof.

One merely has to define by induction
\varepsilon_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
which does not lie in the small special subgroup generated by
B(\alpha_{k})_{k}\varepsilon_{<i}
, and choose
\beta_{i}=\alpha_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}
.
∎

Lemma 7.33
.

Let
N>0
. Suppose we have
f_{1},\ldots f_{N}\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})
, as well as
Y_{1},\ldots Y_{N}\in\bigcup\limits_{m}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.5714pt}{$\displaystyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.47221pt}{$\textstyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.58528pt}{$\scriptstyle{l^{m}M}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{M}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.7986pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{l^{m}M}$}}}
, such that every tuple of
M
which realizes
p_{l}
satisfies the formula
\bigvee\limits_{i}f_{i}(x)\in Y_{i}
. Then at least one of the
Y_{i}
must have a point in
B
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{i}\in Y_{i}
for each
i
. By
lemma

7.32
, we can assume without loss that
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
. Suppose by contradiction that none of the
Y_{i}
has a point in
B
. Then we can apply
lemma

7.31
to
(\alpha_{i})_{i}
, we find
(\beta_{i})_{i}\equiv_{B}(\alpha_{i})_{i}
such that, for all
m>0
and for all
i
,
k
, if
\alpha_{i}\not\in B+l^{m}M
, then
\beta_{i}-\alpha_{k}\not\in l^{m}M
. In particular, as
Y_{k}
does not intersect
B
, we have
\beta_{i}\not\in Y_{k}
for all
i
,
k
. Let
\sigma\in\textup{Aut}({M}/B)
such that
\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\beta_{i}
for each
i
. Then we must have
\left(\bigcup\limits_{i}Y_{i}\right)\cap\left(\bigcup\limits_{i}\sigma(Y_{i})\right)=\emptyset
. However, as
p_{l}
is
\textup{Aut}(M/B)
-invariant, we must have
p_{l}(x)\models\bigvee\limits_{i}f_{i}(x)\in\sigma(Y_{i})
, a contradiction.
∎

Let us recall that we adopted
7.1
in the section.

Proposition 7.34
.

Consider the action of
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
on
Q=S^{n}_{l}(M)
. Let
C
be the group of all
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of
c
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

Some invariant element of
Q
extends
p_{l}
.

(2)

Some element of
Q
of bounded orbit extends
p_{l}
.

(3)

p_{l}
does not fork over
A
.

(4)

p_{l}
does not divide over
A
.

(5)

For each
N>0
, we have
(C+l^{N}M)\cap(B+l^{N}M)=A+l^{N}M
.

Proof.

The directions
1\Longrightarrow 2
and
3\Longrightarrow 4
are trivial.

Let us prove
2\Longrightarrow 3
. For each prime
l^{\prime}\neq l
, we can define
q_{l^{\prime}}
just as in
proposition

7.6
. There remains to show that there exists
q_{<}\in\pi_{<}(F)
whose orbit is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant. In some elementary extension of
{M}
, define by induction
(a_{i})_{i},(D_{i})_{i}
such that
D_{i}
is the special subgroup generated by
Ma_{<i}
, and
a_{i}\in\bigcap\limits_{b\in D_{i}}]b,+\infty[
. Define
q_{<}=\textup{tp}_{<}(a/M)
. Then, for all
(\lambda_{1}\ldots\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and
b\in M
, whether
\sum\limits_{i}\lambda_{i}a_{i}>b
depends uniquely on the sign of
(\lambda_{i})_{i}
in the anti-lexicographic sum
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, and it does not depend on
b
. Moreover,
(a_{i})_{i}
is clearly
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
M
. As a result,
q_{<}
is an
\textup{Aut}({M})
-invariant (and hence
\textup{Aut}({M}/A)
-invariant) element of
\pi_{<}(F)
, and we conclude.

Let us prove
4\Longrightarrow 5
. Let
f\in\textup{LC}(\mathbb{Z})
and
N>0
be such that
f(c)
is in
B+l^{N}M\setminus A+l^{N}M
. Let
b\in B
such that
f(c)-b\in l^{N}M
(we know that
b\not\in A+l^{N}M
by hypothesis, in particular it is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A
as a singleton). Let us show that the formula
\varphi(x,b):=\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}\left(f(x)-b\right)
divides over
A
, which implies that
4
fails. We can repeatedly apply
lemma

7.31
to find
(b_{i})_{i<\omega}
such that
b_{0}=b
,
b_{i}\equiv_{B}b_{i+1}
, and
b_{i}-b_{j}\not\in l^{N}M
for all
j\neq i
. Then the set of formulas
\left\{\varphi(x,b_{i})|i<\omega\right\}
is clearly
2
-inconsistent, so we can conclude.

Let us prove
5\Longrightarrow 1
. Suppose
5
holds, and let us build an explicit invariant element of
Q
which extends
p_{l}
, witnessing
1
. Define:

F=\left\{(f,N)\in\textup{LC}^{n}(\mathbb{Z})\times\omega|f(c)\in A+l^{N}M\right\}

For each
(f,N)\in F
, let
a_{f,N}\in A
such that
f(c)-a_{f,N}\in l^{N}M
. Define the following partial type:

\begin{array}[]{ccl}p(x)&=&\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(f(x)-a_{f,N})|(f,N)\in F\right\}\\
&&\cup\left\{\neg\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}(f(x)-e)|(f,N)\not\in F,e\in M\right\}\end{array}

Suppose by contradiction that
p
is not consistent with
p_{l}
. Then, by compactness, there exists a finite tuple
(f_{i},N_{i},e_{i})_{i}
such that, for every
i
,
(f_{i},N_{i})\not\in F
, and
p_{l}(x)\models\bigvee\limits_{i}\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N_{i}}}(f_{i}(x)-e_{i})
. By
lemma

7.33
applied to
Y_{i}=e_{i}\textup{ mod }l^{N_{i}}M
, there must exist
i
such that
e_{i}\in B+l^{N_{i}}M
. As
(f_{i},N_{i})\not\in F
, we must have
e_{i}\not\in A+l^{N_{i}}M
, thus
e_{i}\in B+l^{N_{i}}M\setminus A+l^{N_{i}}M
. By hypothesis
5
, we have
p_{l}(x)\models\neg\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N_{i}}}(f_{i}(x)-e_{i})
, which implies that
p_{l}(x)\models\bigvee\limits_{k\neq i}\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N_{k}}}(f_{k}(x)-e_{k})
. We keep decreasing by induction the size of this disjunction, until we inevitably reach a contradiction. As a result,
p
must be consistent with
p_{l}
, and it is clearly
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant. This partial type is of course complete as an element of
Q
, because every atomic formula with predicate in
\left\{\mathfrak{d}_{l^{N}}|N>0\right\}
and parameters in
M
either lies in
p
, or its negation lies in
p
.
∎

Remark 7.35
.

When the conditions of
proposition

7.34
hold, the explicit type in the proof of
5\Longrightarrow 1
is actually the unique global extension of
p_{l}
which has a bounded orbit. Indeed, if
q
is another global extension, and
d
is a realization of
q
in some elementary extension, then we must have
f(d)-\alpha\in l^{N}M
for some
(f,N)\not\in F
, and
m\in M
. In that case,
m\not\in A+l^{N}M
, thus the fifth condition from
proposition

7.34
fails with
c,B
replaced by
d,M
. In particular,
q
divides over
A
, thus its orbit is unbounded.

8.
Computation of forking

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
{M}\models
ROAG
, let
A
,
B
be parameter subsets of
M
, let
\kappa=\max(|AB|,2^{\aleph_{0}})^{+}
, and let
c=c_{1}\ldots c_{n}\in M^{n}
. Suppose
{M}
is
\kappa
-saturated and strongly
\kappa
-homogeneous. Let
C
be the subgroup of
M
generated by
c
, and
A^{\prime},B^{\prime}
the special subgroups generated by
A,AB
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

•

c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}B

•

c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}}B

•

c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}}B

•

\textup{tp}(c/AB)
has a global
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant extension.

•

the following conditions hold:

(1)

Every closed bounded interval of
B^{\prime}
that has a point in
C
already has a point in
\textup{div}(A^{\prime})
.

(2)

For all prime
l
, if
[M:lM]
is infinite, then we have, for all
N>0
,
(C+l^{N}M)\cap(B^{\prime}+l^{N}M)=A^{\prime}+l^{N}M
.

Moreover,
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B
if and only if the above conditions hold, and, additionally, for every prime
l
for which
[M:lM]
is finite, we have
C\subseteq A^{\prime}+\bigcap\limits_{N>0}l^{N}M
.

Note that, if
c\underset{A}{{\not\downfree}^{\mathbf{d}}}B
, then
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
divides over
\textup{acl}^{eq}(A)
, thus it does not admit any global
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant extension.

Proof.

Firstly, we have
A^{\prime}=\textup{dcl}(A)
and
B^{\prime}=\textup{dcl}(AB)
by
corollary

6.6
, thus we have
c\underset{A}{\downfree}B
if and only if
c\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree}B^{\prime}
for every
\underset{}{\downfree}\in\{\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}\}
. Secondly, given
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}_{1}\ldots c^{\prime}_{m}
a maximal subtuple of
c
which is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
A^{\prime}
,
c
and
c^{\prime}
are
A
-interdefinable, thus one can show that for all of those independence notions we have
c\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree}B^{\prime}
if and only if
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree}B^{\prime}
. Likewise,
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
has an
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant extension if and only if
\textup{tp}(c^{\prime}/B^{\prime})
has an
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A^{\prime}\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant extension.

If
c^{\prime}
was not
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B^{\prime}
, then one could show that
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{{\not\downfree}^{\mathbf{d}}}B^{\prime}
(hence
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{{\not\downfree}^{\mathbf{f}}}B^{\prime}
,
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{{\not\downfree}^{\mathbf{bo}}}B^{\prime}
, and
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
does not admit any global extension which is
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant). Moreover, condition
1
would fail on some singleton of
B\setminus A
witnessing the fact that
c^{\prime}
is not
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B
.

Suppose
c^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
B^{\prime}
. For each
j\in J
, let
p_{j}=\textup{tp}_{j}(c^{\prime}/B^{\prime})
, and let
F_{j}
be the image by
\pi_{j}
of the space of complete global types with realizations that are
\mathbb{Q}
-free over
M
.

If condition 1 fails, then
p_{<}
divides over
A^{\prime}
by
proposition

7.6
, and if condition 2 fails for some
l
, then
p_{l}
divides over
A^{\prime}
by
proposition

7.34
. Either way, we have
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{{\not\downfree}}B^{\prime}
for every
\underset{}{\downfree}\in\{\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}},\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}\}
.

Suppose conditions 1 and 2 hold, then
p_{<}
extends to an
\textup{Aut}({M}/A^{\prime})
-invariant (thus
\textup{Aut}({M^{eq}}/A^{\prime}\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant) element of
F_{<}
by
proposition

7.6
, and, for every prime
l
for which
[M:lM]
is infinite,
p_{l}
extends to some
\textup{Aut}({M}/A^{\prime})
-invariant extension of
F_{l}
by
7.34
. By
remark

7.21
, for each prime
l
of finite index, any global extension of
p_{l}
is automatically
\textup{Aut}({M^{eq}}/A^{\prime}\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant, thus it follows that
\textup{tp}(c^{\prime}/A^{\prime}B^{\prime})
has a global
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A^{\prime}\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
-invariant extension. Moreover, by
remark

7.21
, we know that
p_{l}
extends to some element of
F_{l}
of bounded orbit under
\textup{Aut}({M}/A^{\prime})
for every prime
l
for which
[M:lM]
is finite. By
corollary

6.7
,
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree^{\mathbf{bo}}}B^{\prime}
, thus
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}B^{\prime},c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree^{\mathbf{d}}}B^{\prime}
. Moreover, by
corollary

6.7
, we have
c^{\prime}\underset{A^{\prime}}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}B^{\prime}
if and only if, for every prime
l
for which
[M:lM]
is finite,
p_{l}
extends to some
\textup{Aut}({M}/A^{\prime})
-invariant element of
F_{l}
. Then we can conclude using
proposition

7.22
.
∎

Corollary 8.2
.

We have
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{Sh}}}
in
ROAG
, where
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{Sh}}}B
, Shelah-independence, is equivalent to
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
having a global extension which is
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/\textup{acl}^{eq}(A))
-invariant.

Proof.

Shelah-invariance is a weaker condition than being invariant under
\textup{Aut}(M^{eq}/A\cup\textup{acl}^{eq}(\emptyset))
, which is weaker than forking in general. The other direction follows from the theorem.
∎

We know (see
remark

1.5
) that in any NIP theory,
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
coincides with
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
over models. We can now refine that result for ROAG:

Corollary 8.3
.

Let
A^{\prime}
be the special subgroup generated by
A
. Then
\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
if and only if
A^{\prime}+\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M=M
for all primes
l
of finite index.

Moreover,
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{inv}}}
if and only if, for each prime
l
, either
M
is
l
-divisible, or the index
[M:lM]
is infinite.

Remark 8.4
.

Suppose that
M
is dense. Then, by
proposition

7.16
,
remark

7.35
,
corollary

7.28
, and
remark

3.31
, we get the three following properties :

•

If
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}B
, then every global extension of
p_{<}
which does not fork over
A
is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant. Moreover, the Stone space of those extensions can be written as
S_{1}
, a finite product of finite coproducts of closed subspaces of
S_{\textup{DOAG}}(\{0\})
.

•

Let
l
be prime such that
[M:lM]
is finite. If
C\subseteq A^{\prime}+\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M
, then
p_{l}
has only one global extension, and it is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant. Else, none of the global extensions of
p_{l}
is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant, but they are all non-forking over
A
, and the space of those extensions can be written
S_{2,l}
, a closed subspace of
\mathbb{Z}_{l}^{|c^{\prime}|}
.

•

Let
l
be prime such that
[M:lM]
is infinite. If
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}B
, then
p_{l}
has only one global extension which does not fork over
A
, and this extension is
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant.

As a result, if we define:

L=\left\{l\textup{ prime }|[M:lM]<\omega,C\not\subseteq A^{\prime}+\bigcap\limits_{N}l^{N}M\right\},

then the space
S
of global extensions of
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
which do not fork over
A
is either empty, or homeomorphic to
S_{1}\times\prod\limits_{l\in L}S_{2,l}
.

Moreover, if
S\neq\emptyset
and
L=\emptyset
, then all the elements of
S
are
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant, thus
S
is homeomorphic to
S_{1}
, and it coincides with the space of global
\textup{Aut}(M/A)
-invariant extensions of
\textup{tp}(c/AB)
.

9.
Examples

9.1.
Non-forking versus finite satisfiability

Let us start with the very basic remark that the cut independence notion does not coincide with finite satisfiability in DOAG. In an elementary extension of the ordered group
\mathbb{R}
, let
\varepsilon>0
,
c>0
such that
\Delta(c)<\Delta(\varepsilon)<\Delta(1)
. Let
A=\mathbb{Q}
,
B=\mathbb{Q}+\mathbb{Q}\varepsilon
. Then we clearly have
c\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{cut}}}B
, but the type of
c
over
B
is not finitely satisfiable over
A
: the
B
-definable set
]0,\varepsilon]
contains
c
and has no point in
A
.

9.2.
Non-forking versus sequential independence

We would like to compare non-forking independence with a stronger independence notion:

Definition 9.1
.

Let
{M}
be any first-order structure, and
A
,
B
,
C\subseteq{M}
parameter sets. We write
C\underset{A}{\downfree^{\mathbf{seq}}}B
when, for any finite tuple
c
from
C
, there exists a finite tuple
d=(d_{0},\ldots d_{n})
from
\textup{acl}(AC)
such that
\textup{acl}(Ac)\subseteq\textup{acl}(Ad)
and
d_{i}\underset{Ad_{\leqslant i}}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}B
for every
i\leqslant n
.

This relation, which we call
sequential independence
, is stronger than
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
in any theory (an easy application of left-transitivity of non-forking). One can show that it coincides with
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
in simple theories, and this is also the case in some non-simple theories, like DLO (a parameter set coincides with its algebraic closure, and it is easy to guess the global invariant extensions of the type of some finite tuple). In a theory where
\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{seq}}}=\underset{}{\downfree^{\mathbf{f}}}
